# Changelog

Vilan is a fast-moving alpha. Minor versions (`0.X`) may change the
language, the standard library, and the wire protocol without a
deprecation period; patch versions are fixes. Each release below links
the highlights — the [book](https://vilan-lang.org/docs/) always
tracks the latest state.

## Unreleased

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The `css` block is in the spec, the tour and the editors now — and a block written without `import std::style::style` says so in its own words.** S2 shipped the guide section its own surface needed and left the rest for this slice. `spec/grammar.md` gains the `css-block` productions beside `element`'s, in the same `atom` block, with the prose that goes with them: the dot decides and decides alone, a property name is the element-name rule's span-adjacent run, the `;` is required after every declaration including the last, a value is tokens and holes with no typed grammar behind it, and the block desugars before analysis to a `std::style` chain — plus the brace-initial exclusion, recorded where the struct initializer's already is (§3.8), because a `css` block is suppressed in a condition, a `for … in` iterable and a `match` subject for exactly the same reason and takes exactly the same escape. `spec/lexical.md` gains two things it was missing: `css` in the reserved-word list, where a hard keyword since S2 had never been written down, and the first statement in that file that a character can fail to lex at all — `#` and `@` are in neither token class, so either one is a lex error wherever it is written, a `css` block included, *because* lexing finishes before any parser exists (§2.5 was already the sentence; this is its consequence). The two costs are stated with their spellings: a colour is a hole through `Color`, which carries its own `:root` line, and a media query is a breakpoint rule. `guide/styling.md` now shows both spellings of one style **in one compiled program** — the docs gate builds it, so the claim that they mint the same classes is checked rather than asserted — and documents what `vilan fmt` does with a block, barriers and the comment refusal included. `tour/coming-from-javascript.md` gains six phrasebook rows and one thing to unlearn (don't type `#` or `@`). The VS Code grammar gains a hand-written `#css` repository rule beside `#elements`: a begin/end over the block so a property name is coloured as a property only inside one, with condition rules taking their own braces and `{expression}` holes falling back to the whole vilan grammar. It is structure, not a generated word list, so it sits outside every `grammar_sync.rs` seam — and the generated `keyword.other` row that colours the word `css` is untouched. Finally, the diagnostic: a block lowers to `style()`, so a missing import fails on the generated accessor, and S2 kept that one accessor's span at the `css` KEYWORD for exactly this. The report was honest but disjointed — `css` underlined, `style` in the message, nothing drawing the line — so it now carries a note that does, on the element-syntax precedent (`element syntax lowers to std::ui::view`), gated on the span reading `css` so it cannot fire on an ordinary unresolved `style`. (proposal/css-block.md §11 S4)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan fmt` prints a `css { … }` block, and orders it exactly as it orders the chain it desugars to.** S2 shipped a verbatim source-slice passthrough — enough to keep the bail set empty, so a file holding a block still formatted — and left the block's interior untouched. It now has a real printer: one item per line, a nested rule's own items one level further in, `{expression}` holes canonicalized as the ordinary vilan expressions they are (`{space( 4 )}` → `{space(4)}`), and blank lines between items dropped. A one-declaration block still collapses onto its line (`let active = const css { padding: {space(6)}; };`); a nested rule never does, because a rule sharing its line with its declarations is not CSS. And the items **sort**: properties in Tailwind's category sequence, then the conditions in the axis order the selector nests them — the same order a `style()` chain gets (proposal/css-block.md §8, Q2 ruled 2026-08-28), reached through the *same order function and the same tables*, so one style cannot have two canonical spellings. What the block deliberately does not get is a table of its own: the rank tables are keyed by METHOD name and a block writes CSS PROPERTY names, so the property rank is derived from the rows' own `properties` column, and `style_table_sync.rs` gates that no property is claimed by two families — the condition that makes the derivation well defined at all. Two differences from the chain, both earned: `raw` is a barrier in a chain because its slot is an argument the formatter cannot evaluate, while in a block the property is a token and really does rank; and a property no `Style` method writes — a vendor property, a custom property — is a barrier that nothing crosses, which is `raw`'s escape hatch surviving into the block whole. Reordering is **refused outright** for any block holding a comment, nested rules included: a permuted body would carry its comments to the wrong item, and the comment cursor only moves forward. The safety net gained the matching token-level pass, so the net and the printer cannot come to disagree about what canonical means, and the external corpus tripwire mirrors it through that one implementation rather than a copy. The corpus program reflowed under the new order, and what moved is the whole argument: **not one byte of emitted CSS changed** — class names are content hashes of the slot and the declaration, so a moved hash would have shown — and the only `.mjs` movement is two `class_list()` strings carrying the same classes in a different insertion order, which is what a reorder is allowed to change. Pinned per form through `assert_construct` (token identity, no silent bail, the canonical form, idempotence, round-trip), with the family and comment rules proven anti-vacuously — plant the plausible mistake and watch the pin redden. (proposal/css-block.md §8 / §11 S3)

## v0.38.0 — 2026-08-28

<!-- family: breaking -->
**An `asset::emit` kind may no longer name a file the build writes itself — `emit("vl", …)` in a bare build OVERWROTE THE ENTRY SOURCE and exited 0.** E94 fenced the kind's SHAPE (one path segment, no `..`, no separator) and stopped there, and shape was half the question: a single segment beside the build output can still be a segment the build writes there itself. A lone package's outputs sit exactly where its entry does, so `emit("vl", "CLOBBERED")` resolved to `app.vl`, the flush wrote it, and the build printed `Emitted  app.vl`, then `Compiled`, and exited 0 with the program's own source replaced by one line. The same hole reached `mjs`/`js` (the compiled bundle — under `vilan run` the canonical `app.mjs` was replaced by the emitted text), `chunks.json` (the build manifest) and the whole `<arm>.js` route-chunk family, and that last is worse than an overwrite: `sweep_stale_chunks` DELETES what lands there, so a kind parked on it does not merely collide, it disappears on the next build. The refusal now sits in the same const-time fence, taken on the EVALUATED kind rather than on the syntax — a computed kind folding to `"vl"` clobbered the source exactly as the literal did, and there is no other route in, since `asset::emit` is const-only — and it reads ONE list, `const_eval::build_owned_emit_kind`, which is the list the per-kind prune (G6) already read to decide what it may delete. One list, two consumers, so the write side and the prune side cannot come to disagree about what the build owns; the sharing is pinned by a plant that breaks the list and reddens both consumers' tests. `css` is the one member the fence admits, because the build owns it AND `emit` is how it is written — the whole styling system reaches `<leg>.css` that way, and `build-hooks.md` §5.6 reserves `css` against a build *hook*, never against the const channel that produces it. **What changes for you:** a program emitting `vl`, `js`, `mjs`, `chunks.json`, or any `<arm>.js` kind stops compiling, with a diagnostic naming which of the build's own files the kind collides with and telling you to pass a kind of the program's own; nothing in std, the corpus, the docs, or the examples emitted one of them. (backlog G7, ruled 2026-08-28; proposal/build-hooks.md §5.6)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`css` is a keyword now — and `Length::css(…)` is `Length::raw(…)`, the `.css` field of a `Length`/`Color` is `.text`.** The word was taken so the `css { … }` block has a grammar seat (proposal/css-block.md, Q3 ruled 2026-08-28). A contextual gate — `css` immediately followed by `{`, in atom position — would have served the headless block at zero cost, but the headed form `css [data-theme="dark"] { … }` collides with indexing a variable named `css` and `css :root` with a path, so it needs a real keyword; and taking one is three renames and a grammar regeneration in alpha, impossible after the v0.40.0 beta contract. The rename is mechanical and the compiler names it at every position the word can appear — a member access (`space(4).css`), a `::` path segment (`Length::css(..)`), a binding, a struct field declaration, a struct-initializer field — each reaching the same curated refusal through a different seam in the parser, because a bare "found `css`, expected an identifier" would leave the reader to guess what their `.css` became. `Length::raw` is the escape-hatch spelling `Style::raw` already uses for the same job (a complete value, verbatim, no `calc(..)` wrapper), and `.text` is what `CssValue::css_text` was already calling it. Nothing else moves: every emitted class name, every `:root` line and every corpus golden is byte-identical, because `Length` and `Color` are const-folded and their field name never reaches the emitted JavaScript. The three-place keyword rule is paid by generation as ever — one `KEYWORDS` row, one `Token` variant, one `KEYWORD_ROLES` row, then `VILAN_REGENERATE_GRAMMARS=1`. (proposal/css-block.md §5.4/§12 Q3; docs `guide/styling.md` and `std/style.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`fs::exists` is deleted — probe with `stat(path).is_some()`.** Its stated justification named a category ("so boot code can branch on it directly") rather than a caller, and the standing rule for this surface (proposal/filesystem.md §4, minted after the sync-read claim that turned out to be false the day it was written) demands a name a later reader can look up. Looked up: all three callers in the tree — two in `std::build`, one in the todo example — were async functions already awaiting file reads a line or two later, so the one thing `exists` offered over `stat(path).is_some()`, its syncness, served nobody, and it blocked the event loop for a full stat to do it; `stat` also answers strictly more, with the same non-throwing posture on a missing path. Migration is mechanical: `if fs::exists(p)` → `if fs::stat(p).is_some()`, `if !fs::exists(p)` → `if fs::stat(p).is_none()` — and where the question was really "then act on it", the new `File` handle answers without the TOCTOU race `exists`-then-act always was. With it gone `std::fs` is async end to end; the rule stands for anything that would come back. (kolt.local 031 Q3, ruled 2026-08-27; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`Style::dark` is deleted; the theme condition is `within("data-theme", "dark", ..)`.** A census across the whole estate found zero user `.dark(` calls — the website, the examples and kolt all theme through tokens or their own systems — so a deprecation window would have protected nobody, and the ruling (kolt.local 014, 2026-08-27) is delete rather than deprecate. The replacement is strictly more general: `within` guards on ANY ancestor attribute, so the binary `dark` becomes one spelling among n-ary theme ids (`within("data-theme", "iron-dark", ..)`), composing with `attribute`, pseudo-classes and breakpoints exactly where `dark` composed (`md(within(.., hover(..)))`) and refusing the same misnestings by name. What moves in the emitted CSS: the ancestor selector drops its `:root` (`[data-theme="dark"] .sX`, specificity (0,2,0) where dark's was (0,3,0)); the recorded outcomes survive — a theme still beats a state on a shared property, composed rules still beat their parts — with one stated flip: a plain guard no longer beats `md(hover(..))` on a shared property, and `md(within(hover(..)))` is the spelling that says so (proposal/ui-styling.md §0bis.6's ledger). Every class name outside the old dark band is byte-identical.

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An `asset::emit` reached only through trait dispatch escaped the const-only check — a clean compile, then a `ReferenceError` at run time.** The check propagates "compile-time-only" over call edges, and a bounded generic's `value.text()` has none: an emitting impl behind it was invisible, so while both concrete spellings of the same call were refused, the generic one landed in the emitted JavaScript as a live `__emit_asset(...)` with no runtime binding. The check now follows the analyzer's recorded dispatch — the same refinement the context coverage check uses, extracted into a shared `dispatch_refine` module: a generic call is charged at the entry whose substitution selects an emitting impl, so the refusal is per call site and a clean impl of the same trait member through the same generic stays legal; an inherited trait default re-dispatched onto a concrete receiver narrows by the receiver's head; module-level initializer entries are boundaries like any other; and anything the compiler cannot resolve — a shared default body's `self` call — refuses every receiver, conservatively, because the alternative is the silent crash. Inside a `const` nothing changes: the interpreter makes the call, which is the whole styling shape. Pinned in both directions, with plants confirming the escape pins and the clean-admission pins each redden alone. (backlog B143)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A build hook can say what it reads and writes, and then stop running.** `[build] run` executes every command on every build and every `--watch` round, which is right for a Tailwind pass and wrong for anything that downloads or generates at scale — the motivating case (a script that fetches an icon set and writes a package's worth of modules) paid its full cost per keystroke. A hook that should run only when something moved now gets a name and a declaration: `[[build.hook]]` with `name`, `run`, and optional `inputs` / `outputs`, running after every `run = [...]` command in declaration order. It is **fresh** — skipped, with a dim `Fresh <name>` line rather than silence — when it has a stamp, every declared output exists and re-digests to its recorded value, every declared input re-digests to its recorded value, and the command string is unchanged; otherwise it runs. **Content, never mtime**, on the watch loop's own recorded ground: rewriting a file with the same bytes is not a change, and a stamp that trusted timestamps would reintroduce the bug leg reuse already refused. Three details are decisions rather than defaults. A declared input that is **missing** is recorded as missing, so its later appearance invalidates — the same posture `asset::read`'s reader takes to its misses. A declared path is matched **literally**: a file, or a directory (which digests its whole tree, so `inputs = ["src/static"]` means what it looks like), and a glob such as `src/**/*.css` is a manifest error naming the fix, because an unmatched pattern would digest as a file that is never there and freeze the hook after its first run. And a hook that declares **neither** `inputs` nor `outputs` is never fresh and always runs — which is today's behavior, so `run = [...]` is unchanged to the byte and a `run`-only project grows no new files. The stamp lives at `dist/.build-hooks.json` in the project's own directory, not a machine-global cache: `rm -rf dist` means rebuild everything, hooks included, and `vilan build --rerun-hooks` is the one-off form. Every failure to read it — a truncation, a hand edit, a version this binary does not know — reads as *no stamp*, so the worst a broken stamp can do is cost the hook runs the toolchain would have paid anyway; a hook that fails records nothing, so the next build re-runs it. The staleness is over *declared* inputs and is knowingly unsound for a hook that reads what it did not declare (the failure is a stale artifact, never a wrong program — the compiler still hashes what it actually read), and skipping is a cost optimization over code already trusted to run, never a containment property. (proposal/build-hooks.md §3 / S1, the owner's Q2 ruling of 2026-08-28; docs `guide/dev-loop.md`, `spec/platform.md` §11.4, `appendix/cli.md`, `tour/projects.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`const preflight()` — an opinionated base stylesheet, opt-in and opt-out-able by construction.** std shipped no reset at all: UA defaults were fully in force, `body` kept its 8px margin, `box-sizing` was content-box everywhere so `width(px(200)).padding(space(4))` measured 232px, and no page said so — every app hand-shipped a `preflight.css` and served it itself. `preflight()` is Tailwind's reset-nearly-everything scope adapted: the universal border-box, the UA's margins zeroed, its form-control chrome stripped so a button inherits the page's font and colour instead of the platform's, replaced elements laid out as blocks that cannot overflow their container, list markers and heading sizes gone — **plus one rule no reset it is adapted from carries: `a`, `button` and `select` are `display: block`** (the ruling's own addition, and the most visible thing in it — an anchor in prose becomes a block, so a page that wants inline links styles them back). It is a std style asset the app EMITS, not a `Document` option or a build flag: the only door into the stylesheet is a `const` expression, so one line opts in, deleting it opts out, and a build that never calls it emits a byte-identical sheet — which also keeps it available to a browser-only project, the one that wants a reset most and has no server leg to configure. **Ordering was the design risk, and it is settled by the cascade rather than by the sheet's sort.** Every reset rule emits inside `@layer vilan.preflight`, a *sub-layer* of the layer declaration blocks use, so one sentence of css-cascade-5 §6.4.3 — unlayered rules sort after layered ones within the same parent layer — applies twice in the same direction: every `Style` beats every reset rule whatever the specificity (the guarantee `declare` already gave, unchanged), and every `declare` block beats every reset rule too, so an app tightening `a` writes `declare("a", …)` and needs neither `!important` nor a longer selector. The reset is the weakest author-origin thing in the sheet and still stronger than every UA default, because layers order *author* declarations only. Nothing about any of that reads a byte offset, which matters because the sheet's order is a lexical sort with no notion of a reset. Pinned four ways — opt-in emits, opt-out emits nothing and leaves the atomic sheet byte-identical, the three cascade tiers proven on one property, and a new `preflight.vl` corpus program holding every rule and its layer to the byte — each proven red first, and no existing golden moved. (kolt.local 008, ruled 2026-08-28; docs `std/style.md` and `guide/styling.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`serve_build` gained a caching hook — opt-in, one line, and nothing changes for a server that does not call it.** The artifacts a build serves re-downloaded on every page load, and the two-tier policy every static layer converges on was spellable everywhere except where it was wanted: `std::http` shipped `etag_of`/`etag_response` (v0.38.0) for a page you render yourself, but `serve_build`'s asset routes sent one `Content-Type` header and the bytes, so the motivating server hand-rolled its own static layer and recorded its ETag surrender as a comment. `ServerBuilder::cache_build(|url| CachePolicy)` is the opt-in: it asks your policy, per artifact, what to send beyond the bytes — `CachePolicy::validated()` for a validator and its `304`, `CachePolicy::none()` for neither, `.cache_control(value)` for the field value verbatim on either base — so the fingerprinted-plus-revalidated pair is one expression on the chain a server already writes. Composition rather than re-derivation: `validated()` *is* `etag_of` + `etag_response` over the artifact's own bytes, and the still-open `ResponseBuilder` those helpers were deliberately given is what carries `Cache-Control` onto the `304` arm as well as the `200`, exactly as RFC 9110 §15.4.5 asks. The policy is a function of the **url** and of nothing else — "which of my build's files is this?" is the whole question a two-tier policy asks, and keeping the request out is what stops a caching policy from quietly becoming a second request handler behind `serve_build`'s back; anything genuinely per-request stays `on_request`'s, built from `etag_response` directly. The validator is minted over the bytes actually being served, taken once, so it stays right under `run --watch` where each artifact is re-read per request — one sha-256 per hit on a validating route, which is the trade `etag_of` documents and a boot-time digest could not make. **The default is untouched, and by control flow rather than by arithmetic:** with no policy on the chain the asset route calls the same function it always did, so the un-opted response is byte-identical because of which branch runs, not because a neutral policy happens to add nothing — pinned as a header-set equality on a default server, proven red by planting the hook into the default arm. `fullstack-dx.md` §5.10's fence stands as written: `serve_build` still serves a build and not a directory, still honours no `Range`, and still has no caching default at all. (kolt.local 025b, ruled 2026-08-28; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A `css { … }` block is a `Style`, written as CSS.** `style().raw("display", "flex").hover(style().raw("background-color", Color::gray(100)))` now also spells as `css { display: flex; .hover { background-color: {Color::gray(100)}; } }` — the element syntax's twin on the style side, and sugar over the chain in exactly the sense markup is sugar over the `view` chain: a pure pre-analysis `Node → Node` desugar running in the same slot, at every parse entry (macro-expansion output included). **There is no third emission channel and not one byte of change to the atomic model** — `Style::rule` is still the one chokepoint, `class_hash`, `render_rule`, the family table, the `@media` sort band and the `@layer vilan` rule are untouched — and the arc's headline gate is what that buys: the same program written as a block and as the chain it desugars to emits **byte-identical CSS and byte-identical JS**, pinned both ways (the CSS half alone would miss a reordering, because the sheet is an over-approximation; the JS half catches which slots actually resolved). **One rule, and the feature falls out of it:** an undotted `property: value;` is a declaration and lowers to `.raw(property, value)`; a dotted `.name { … }` is a condition combinator and lowers to `.name(style() … )` with the block's own chain last. The dot is the whole disambiguator, so the desugar is NAME-BLIND — the grammar never consults `Style`'s method list, every combinator that exists works on the day it ships (`.within`, `.children`, `.divide` included), nesting order is combinator order, and a method added to `Style` can never change what existing `css` means. Values are token runs and `{expression}` holes: a value that is *exactly* one hole passes its expression through untouched, so `gap: {space(4)};` keeps a `Length` and the `:root` line the sheet needs beside it; anything else is the value's own source slice as a `str`, holes interpolated into the very tree an i-string builds. Written order is preserved; nothing is reordered, deduped or merged at lowering. Four refusals, each naming its fix: `#` and `@` are in no charset and cannot lex (lexing is context-free by spec, so a block cannot make them lex there either) and now carry curated rules pointing at `{Color::hex("#333")}` and `.md { … }`; `!important` is refused permanently, because merging a style is a record update and a later declaration already wins; and the `;` is required after every declaration, including the last, reported as the missing terminator the editor already has an "Insert `;`" quickfix for. A block is brace-initial, so unlike an element it IS suppressed in a condition, a `for … in` iterable and a `match` subject exactly as a struct literal is — the refusal says to parenthesize. `vilan fmt` prints a block back verbatim this slice (the canonical printer is the next one), which is what keeps the formatter's bail set asserted-empty rather than silently returning every file holding a block unformatted. And the spans are cut NOW, not repaired later: every generated scaffolding accessor is zero-width and the AST carries the property-name, value and per-declaration spans whether this slice uses them or not — the one mistake the plan was shaped to avoid, after element syntax paid for it in its LSP slice. (kolt.local 016 S2, proposal/css-block.md §4/§5/§7.3; corpus `css-block.vl`; docs `guide/styling.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::fs` grows its handle tier: `File`, positional reads and writes, `fsync`, and `with_file`.** An open file is now a first-class value — a `resource` on the `Database` template exactly: it moves rather than copies (a stale handle is a compile error, not an `EBADF`), a closure cannot capture it, `List` cannot hold it (`Option<File>` is the sanctioned container, so a pool of open files is not expressible yet), and its destructor closes the host handle at the owner's scope end, with `drop(file)` as the early form and **no public `close()` to fall out of sync with it**. Construction is five named constructors rather than node's flags string — `open` ("r"), `create` ("w"), `create_new` ("wx", the exclusive create a lockfile is built from), `append_to` ("a", where the host makes every write land at the end, position ignored — kept, not papered over), `modify` ("r+") — because `"wx"` is an untyped eleven-member enum whose failure mode is a runtime `EINVAL`. Reads and writes are positional and stateless by design (`read_at`/`write_at` at an explicit byte position; the host's hidden-cursor mode is deliberately unbound, so two loans of one handle cannot interleave through invisible state), `file.stat()` answers with no `Option` and no TOCTOU window (the handle addresses the open file — remove the path and it still reads), `truncate` shrinks or zero-extends, and `sync`/`data_sync` are `fsync`/`fdatasync`: the durability step `write_atomic`'s own comment always admitted it could not give. One semantic is deliberately novel and deliberately narrow (kolt.local 031 Q1, ruled 2026-08-27): the host's `FileHandle.close()` is async and a destructor cannot await, so a dropped `File` *initiates* its close without waiting — written data is safe, the process cannot exit under it, and only the close's own error goes unobserved — while `with_file(path, |file| …)`, the documented idiom, *awaits* the close, making it the one spelling where a failure to close is a failure of the call. The exception is scoped to `File` alone; "drop is synchronous" stays the law. The tier's intended idiom — `File::open(path).read_at(buffer, 0)`, a postfix straight off an implicitly-awaited call — is exactly the shape B141 miscompiled to `undefined` until v0.37.0, which is why the tier waited for that fix; both historically-broken spellings are now pinned positive, corpus and runtime. One `Database` idiom does not carry over: every `File` constructor is async and a module-level `let` cannot await, so a process-lifetime handle is a local in `main`, not a module global. (kolt.local 031 S3, proposal/filesystem.md §3.2/§5; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::fs` can watch: `Watcher`, `Change`, and a pull-shaped `next()`.** There was no watch API at all, and `std::watch` was a false friend — it is the dev-refresh channel (am I a `--watch` child; tell browsers to reload), not a file watcher — so std's own answer to "watch a file" was a comment telling you to poll `stat` yourself. Now `Watcher::watch(path)` observes a path and a directory's immediate entries, `Watcher::watch_all(path)` the whole tree beneath it (`read_dir`/`read_dir_all`'s reach, and `_all` means the same thing here), and `watcher.next()` hands back the next `Change` — a `path` already joined and ready for `read_file_to_str`, plus one of `Created`/`Modified`/`Removed`. **The mechanism is a 300 ms stat-diffing poll, not the host's `fs.watch`, and that is the design rather than an expedient:** node's binding coalesces events and also duplicates them, folds creation, deletion and renaming into one `"rename"` you must `stat` to disambiguate, varies by platform and version on recursive watching, can report a null filename on macOS, and throws outright on a path that does not exist yet — so a watcher built on it could promise only "something under here changed, go look", which is why the design sketch this replaces carried a deliberately impoverished two-variant kind. Comparing two stats promises strictly more on every platform for free, it is the choice the compiler made for its own `--watch` loop in 2026-07, and it lets a watch start on a path that is not there and report its arrival. The costs are stated in the surface rather than discovered: up to an interval of latency, no report at all for a change that cancels itself out inside one interval, blindness to a rewrite that alters neither size nor mtime, and one `stat` per watched entry per interval. A rename arrives as `Removed` plus `Created`, which is what a rename is to an observer comparing two listings; a directory reports `Created` and `Removed` but never `Modified`, because its mtime moves whenever its contents do and those entry changes are already reported one by one. **The shape is a pull, and the language decides that**: a `|Change| void` callback is a *void* channel, so a handler could not await the read of the file it was just told about, and R9 forbids it from capturing a `File` to hold one open across events — `next()` returns into a scope that can already own a handle, and reads exactly like `std::fetch`'s `BodyReader` chunk loop. `Watcher` is a `resource` built on `File`'s model (it moves, no closure captures it, no `List` holds it, no public `stop()`), with one difference that is the point: **its teardown is genuinely synchronous** — stopping a poll is a `clearTimeout` — so it inherits none of `File`'s asynchronous-close exception, which was ruled for `File` alone. That destructor is load-bearing rather than tidy, and the pins prove it: a pending host timer holds the event loop open, so with `impl Watcher with Drop` removed the end-to-end probe runs to its last line and then hangs forever. Nine runtime pins against real file activity, thirteen on the semantics and the emission, and a `watch.vl` corpus golden holding the poll helper's bytes. (kolt.local 020, which the owner ruled owns the whole watch surface on 2026-08-28; proposal/filesystem.md §8 is now a cross-reference to it; docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::http` answers conditional requests: `etag_of`, `etag_response`, `if_none_match_matches`.** The two-tier cache policy every static layer converges on was only half-spellable: `Request::header` (v0.37.0) could read `If-None-Match` and the response side could say `304`, but the validator itself and the RFC bookkeeping in between were every app's own project — the motivating exhibit is a real server that shipped its surrender as a comment. Now `etag_of(bytes)` mints a strong validator (the first 32 hex digits of the body's sha-256, quotes included; colliding with a body you did not choose is still a second preimage, at half the header weight of the full digest), and `etag_response(request, tag, bytes, content_type)` answers the conditional GET whole: `304` with the tag echoed and no body when `If-None-Match` already names it, the full `200` with `ETag` and `Content-Type` otherwise — returned as a still-open `ResponseBuilder`, so `Cache-Control` and friends chain after it and land on whichever arm answered, which is exactly what RFC 9110 asks of a 304. Matching is the RFC's: the single, list and `*` forms, weak comparison on both sides (a proxy-weakened `W/` tag still revalidates), and only `GET`/`HEAD` revalidate — on any other method a matching `If-None-Match` means `412`, a state-changing route's protocol, so there the header is left unconsulted and the bytes go out. `if_none_match_matches(header, etag)` is the comparison half on its own, for a handler building both arms itself. User-land composable only, deliberately: `serve_build`'s default caching policy stays behind the fullstack-dx §5.10 fence (kolt.local 025b, the owner's call), and nothing here touches `serve_build`'s headers. Verified against a real node server end to end — match, mismatch, absent, list, `*`, weak, and the POST gate — each pin proven red first. (kolt.local 025c)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**Styles gained the relation axis — `within(attr, value, inner)`, `children(inner)`, `divide(inner)`.** The axis answers "whose element does this slot dress?" (kolt.local 009+014, proposal/ui-styling.md §0bis.6): `within` guards the element's own slots on an ancestor's attribute (the theme condition above, and any other ancestor state); `children` styles every direct child (`.sX > *`); `divide` every child but the first — rendered `.sX > :not(:first-child)`, the same element set as Tailwind's owl but carrying (0,2,0), so where both relations touch one property `divide` outranks `children` by specificity, never by the sheet's class-hash order. Child-side rules emit inside `@layer vilan`, and that is the whole cascade story: **a child's own `Style` always wins against a rule reaching in from an ancestor**, whatever the specificity — the relations set defaults a child may refuse, and symmetrically cannot override any unlayered CSS. One relation per slot, one contribution per (relation, property) slot, the same last-wins merge, and every pre-existing class name is byte-identical because the relation rides the condition grammar rather than widening the slot key. kolt-shaped theming — per-theme `declare` blocks with var()-backed colors plus a `within` guard — is pinned as the `theme.vl` corpus program, and `vilan fmt` sorts the new combinators on their axis (media, then the relation, then `attribute`, then the pseudo-classes).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A dependency that declares build hooks is now named out loud instead of passed over in silence — and `build-hooks = true` is the line that would grant it.** Only the addressed manifest ever contributed hooks, which is what keeps first-party build code a *first-party* trust tier; what it never did was **say so**. A path dependency declaring `run = ["echo hi"]` produced no output, no warning, and no note, and from the terminal "the policy refused it" and "the toolchain never looked" are the same thing: one reader debugs the dependency, their shell and their PATH before suspecting a rule nobody told them about, and the other never learns that a package in their graph is asking to execute code on their machine. Every dependency in the resolved graph whose own manifest declares a hook now gets one dim `note:` line, once per build, naming it — recorded during resolution, the one pass that already reads every dependency manifest, and deduplicated by package so two workspace members depending on one library say it once. A **note, never a warning**, in either case, because the refusal is a normal outcome and not an error to be dismissed; the exit code is unchanged. Alongside it, tier 2's opt-in gets its spelling: `build-hooks = true` on a dependency's own declaration, beside where the dependency comes from — per dependency (there is no project-wide switch to reconstruct the default it replaces), recorded in the manifest (a word in the diff that a review sees and a revert undoes), and absent means no, with `false` accepted as the same answer written down deliberately. It sits on the declaration rather than in a grant table of its own for two reasons that are properties: you cannot grant what you do not declare, and removing the dependency removes the grant. **Nothing honors it yet, deliberately** — every dependency hook is refused, opted in or not, and the opted-in case gets its own line saying so. The threshold was moved here rather than left to a registry because third-party code already reaches the machine on an ordinary `vilan build` (a git dependency is fetched and compiled), so the only thing holding the line is one function reading one manifest; the point of shipping the key now is that the syntax is fixed and reviewable *before* anything can cross it, and a registry inherits a spelling instead of minting one under pressure. (proposal/build-hooks.md §4.3 / S2 and proposal/build-trust.md §3–§4, the owner's Q4 and Q6 rulings of 2026-08-28)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan run` now sweeps the stale style sidecar, as `vilan build` always did.** Delete the last style from a program and build it, and `<entry>.css` goes away; do the same and `vilan run` it, and the file stayed on disk with the old bytes — round after round under `--watch`, which is the resurrection shape (a served page keeps injecting a stylesheet the source no longer produces) that `sweep_stale_sidecar` exists to prevent. The cause was placement rather than logic: the sweep hung off `write_chunks`, which `build` calls and which `run` and the single-file watch round do not. It now lives in `write_assets` — the flush itself, the one place that knows what this round emitted, and where G6's per-kind prune already sat — so every path that flushes assets prunes through both mechanisms together and `run` answers exactly as `build` does; `write_chunks` keeps its own call for the HMR dev loop, which writes its sidecar straight into `dist/` rather than through the flush. The sweep still names exactly one file, `<entry>.css`, so an unrelated user file beside the entry is not in its reach — pinned, along with the `run` round and a bounded single-file watch round. (backlog G8)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A reconnect whose re-attach is refused now closes the connection, instead of leaving every mirror bound to a dead channel while the socket says it is fine.** When a dropped socket comes back, the generated client re-verifies the contract and calls `__attach` to mint fresh channel ids for its mirrors. The contract half was handled loudly — a server that redeployed a different surface takes the connection to the terminal `Closed` rather than feeding typed mirrors a shape they were not built for — but the `__attach` half's failure arm was empty: `Err(let _failed) => {}`. The rebind loop was skipped in silence, so `connection_state()` reported `Connected` while every `RemoteSource` still pointed at the *previous* connection's channel ids. Nothing on that socket could ever update again, and nothing said so — no signal, no error, no log — so an app's `or`/state machinery had nothing to act on: the one state it can see said the connection was healthy. A refused `__attach` now takes exactly the drift branch's treatment, `Closed` plus `close()`, on the same reasoning: a mirror that cannot be rebound is a desync, not a delay. An UNREACHABLE server during the re-attach is still left alone, deliberately and as before — that socket is already dying and the next drop cycle redials it. What changes for an app that meets this: rpc calls that used to keep working over the live-but-wedged socket now fail fast with `Transport("not connected")`, which is the honest report, since the mirrors beside them were already dead. The path had zero coverage; it has three end-to-end tests now, over real servers — the restarted server is the same service (the mirrors resync), the same service refusing to open a session (`__attach` fails), or a different service (the contract drifts) — driven from one harness where the failure mode is a spawn argument, and reachable entirely through `Service`'s own public surface, so std carries no test-only seam. The new pin was proven red against the empty arm, and the drift sibling — live all along, pinned only at connect time — gained the reconnect-time pin it never had. Docs `std/rpc.md` and `guide/services.md`. (backlog A26, N16 audit run 2, ruled 2026-08-28; `proposal/transport-robustness.md` §2.5)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Every lock in the workspace recovers from poisoning, and a test holds the line.** `vilan-core`'s `parse_clean_cache_clear` took its two process-global mutexes with `PoisonError::into_inner` and the function thirty lines below took *the same two mutexes* with `.unwrap()` — one file recording a defensive belief and contradicting itself, with the five macro-world caches, the analyzer's name intern, the playground's entry intern and the dev channel's client registry all on the undefended side. The defect was the drift, not the missing defense: none of those guards is held across panic-prone code today, so poisoning was not reachable, but the fenced pipeline is exactly the architecture where a *caught* panic outlives the request that raised it, and the consequence is asymmetric — one poisoned process-global turns a one-shot compiler bug into a language server that answers "internal error: the compiler panicked" for the rest of the session. One posture now, uniformly: `.lock()` / `.read()` / `.write()` is followed by `.unwrap_or_else(std::sync::PoisonError::into_inner)` at every site in every crate's `src/`, with the reason written at each. Recovery is only sound because of a property the audit checked site by site — every one of these guards is held over a whole-value insert, the value built and (where it leaks) leaked *before* the lock is taken, so a recovered guard sees an entry that is complete or absent, never torn. One site failed that check and was fixed rather than merely defended: the language server's publish planner holds its guard across `diagnostic_groups`' span arithmetic, so a caught panic there could leave one document's superseded diagnostics in the planner to be folded into every *other* document's republishes of a shared module, with nothing to trigger a correction — `plan_publish` now takes the owner's entry out before recomputing it, so an unwind leaves that owner contributing nothing and the next analysis rebuilds it. Pinned two ways: a poisoned parse cache is proven to serve the identical cached pointer afterwards (poisoned the way a caught panic poisons — an unwind out of a held guard, caught, process still running), and a source scan fails on the next `.unwrap()`-ing lock anyone adds anywhere in the workspace. Both proven red first. (backlog E97, ruled 2026-08-28)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The 69,158-line `tests/inference.rs` is now fifteen subject modules of one test binary.** It was the file every lane appended to and therefore the file every merge fought over — a single append-only surface holding 2,687 pins, where two lanes adding a test in the same cycle collided on the tail by construction. It is now `crates/vilan-core/tests/inference/`: `main.rs` declaring the subjects, `support.rs` holding the shared harness (`assert_compiles`, `assert_compiles_and_runs`, `assert_fails` and the compile drivers they ride on), and fifteen subject modules — `borrows`, `generics`, `returns`, `macros`, `bounds`, `styling`, `platform`, `tuples`, `resources`, `modules`, `std_surface`, `iterators`, `backed_enums`, `markdown`, `hmr` — cut on the file's own section boundaries, the largest 6.7k lines where the old file was ten times that. **One binary, deliberately:** each top-level file under `tests/` links the whole crate (`suite-speed.md` E21), so fifteen top-level files would have bought fourteen extra link steps to buy back the merge conflicts; a `mod` splits the file without splitting the binary. Nothing about running it changes — `cargo test -p vilan-core --test inference` is the same command against the same 2,685 passing and 2 ignored tests, verified name-for-name before and after — and the only visible difference is that nextest's full test id now carries the module (`vilan-core::inference borrows::…` rather than `vilan-core::inference …`), so an id copied verbatim from an old log needs its module prefix while a `-E 'test(name)'` filter is unaffected. Test bodies are untouched: the split is a pure move, proven by a line-multiset comparison of the old file against the new ones, whose only difference is one harness signature that `cargo fmt` rewrapped when `pub` pushed it past the column limit. (backlog B145, first half)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`fs::read_dir_all` hands back `/`-separated entries on Windows, like everywhere else.** node's recursive `readdir` joins nested entries with the *platform* separator, so a Windows entry arrived as `sub\c.txt` — one component to `std::path`, which is `/`-shaped by ruling because a separator-aware path module would make every derived cache key, asset url and golden host-dependent (kolt.local 017). This was the last red test on the Windows CI leg, and it was a real behavioural split rather than a test artefact: the same program listed a directory differently on two hosts. Normalized in the glue rather than in `read_dir_all`, so the whole language stays on one path shape and the one place a host hands back a joined path is the one place that fixes it. **Gated on `path.sep`, deliberately, and this is the interesting half:** a backslash is a *legal filename byte* on Unix, so an unconditional rewrite would corrupt a real file to fix a problem Unix does not have. On Windows a filename cannot contain one, so the split is unambiguous exactly where it runs. Pinned in both directions — the recursive listing's shape, and a Unix name containing a backslash surviving untouched, the latter proven by planting the unconditional form and watching `od\d.txt` become `od/d.txt`. (backlog N25)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Find References asked in the file that DEFINES a symbol now sees the open files that import it — and rename rewrites them.** A references query answered over the open file's own program: its entry plus the import closure *below* it. Reverse dependencies did not exist for that query, so clicking `struct Point` where it is declared returned the declaration and nothing else — and in a multi-file app you are standing in a definer most of the time, which is what "Find References intermittently returns nothing" was from the user's side (kolt.local 034, the last open branch of 003). The importers' programs had already indexed every one of those occurrences; what was missing was a definition identity that survives the program boundary, since each open document analyzes its own program and entity ids are not comparable across them. The identity every program agrees on is the declaration itself — its file and its name span in that file's text — and the language server now resolves the cursor to that key and unions the identifier-occurrence answer over every open document whose program loaded the defining file, deduplicated because the declaration is a row in each of them. Rename reads the same union rather than a parallel mechanism, so a rename issued at the definition rewrites the importers' uses too — their import leaves included — with each contributing program passing the same completeness checks the single-file rename always ran, and one new refusal: an importer whose buffer is ahead of its analysis refuses the rename for the debounce it takes to catch up, instead of guessing at positions or silently emitting the partial edit set the rename rules forbid. (kolt.local 034)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Formatting is a gate now: `cargo fmt --all --check` runs in CI, on a pinned toolchain.** `cargo fmt` after every Rust change is this repository's own rule, and the tree had still drifted four hunks out of true — enough that three lanes in one cycle each hit the churn, reverted it, and reported it. The drift is reformatted away (whitespace-only, proven by comparing the whitespace-stripped byte streams before and after), and a `fmt` job in `ci.yml` holds the line from here, feeding the required `check` like every other leg. What makes the check *stable* is the new `rust-toolchain.toml`: rustfmt's default layout moves between stables, so the gate runs the exact pinned toolchain — the stable the tree builds with today — and a toolchain bump becomes a deliberate commit that lands the pin and its reformat together, rather than a surprise for whoever runs `cargo fmt` next. The suite and release-build legs deliberately keep proving the tree against *current* stable: they declare `RUSTUP_TOOLCHAIN=stable` explicitly, because the toolchain file would otherwise outrank the toolchain their action installs — and on the cross-compiling legs, silently drop their targets. (backlog N21; the clippy and cargo-audit legs the item also names are follow-up, not part of this change)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Non-`css` `emit` kinds order lexically by line, not by the CSS cascade.** The asset flush applied its one comparator — `(Option<min-width>, line)`, the CSS cascade rule with B35's ascending-width override — to *every* accumulated kind, so a kind named `manifest` holding a line that happened to parse as a media rule had that line forced last whatever its first byte: a plain line starting `z` (0x7A) sorted before an `@media` line (`@` is 0x40). The comparator is now fenced to the one kind whose semantics justify it. `css` keeps its cascade order byte-for-byte (the corpus gate holds every golden unchanged), and every other kind sorts lexically by line — the kind-specific rule `const-eval.md` §3 promised, a pure function of the set of contributions, and exactly the order the proposed `emit_keyed(kind, line, line)` desugar would give an un-keyed `emit`, so these bytes hold if that surface lands. Found by Order 16's build-hooks probes (P2). (backlog G5)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A release can no longer be cut over CI that is not verifiably green.** v0.37.0 was tagged and published while its Windows CI leg had been red for days: `release.yml`'s gate ran the full suite on ubuntu only while `ci.yml` tests a ubuntu+windows matrix — so the gate authorizing five publish channels was strictly weaker than the gate deciding a commit is green — and nothing in the cut sequence said to check CI on the commit being tagged. Both halves are fixed. The release gate now runs the same platform matrix as `ci.yml`, extending the "same instrument, change them together" rule from the command to the platforms it runs on; and `scripts/cut-release.sh` reads `ci.yml`'s verdict on origin at the exact commit the cut is against (`gh run list` at that sha — the trustworthy read, where the Pages builds endpoint lags) and REFUSES anything but a completed green run, each state named with its remedy: red says fix the tree, pending says wait, absent says push and let CI finish — and *cannot look* (no `gh`, no `origin`, unreachable) is its own refusal rather than a silent pass, because unverified is not green. `--allow-red-ci` is the deliberate override for the day the gate itself is what is broken, and it prints what it is riding over, loudly. The script refusing rather than the human remembering is the same lesson the orphan-marker refusal taught, applied to CI; every verdict is pinned, the override and the tool-missing refusal included. (backlog L17; releases.md §7 step 1 and §7.2 step 4 record the finding)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A kind that stops emitting stops shipping.** Accumulator flushes write one `<leg>.<kind>` file per kind — and a build whose flush held no contributions for a kind a previous build wrote left the old file in place, so `dist/` kept serving output no current build produced (probe P8: delete the last `emit("routes", …)` and `dist/server.routes` survived, unchanged and unmentioned — worse than a missing file under "a built app needs nothing but `dist/`", because it ships). Every flush now records the kind files it wrote (`.vilan-asset-kinds` beside the outputs, removed with its last entry) and the next build prunes a recorded file whose kind emitted nothing. The pruner acts only on its own record — never on a filename it merely finds, so user-placed files and other legs' outputs are untouched — and the general "delete whatever this build did not write" sweep stays filed with E92. Every build path shares the one helper: `build`, `run`, watch rounds, and the dev loop's `dist/` writes. (backlog G6)

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`walk_type_node`'s catch-all no longer panics — an unclassified node in type position draws a diagnostic.** The analyzer's twelve-variant match on user-input-driven `Node` in type position ended in `unimplemented!` where every neighbouring path degrades into a diagnostic. Probed unreachable today — the type grammar's single door produces only the handled shapes, and B142's `Node::Error` depth stand-in never survives to the walk — so this is hardening, not a live crash: the next type-position variant now draws "this expression cannot be used as a type; write a type here — …" and the slot degrades to the non-cascading `Unresolved`, the same rule already-errored expressions follow, while a parse-recovery `Node::Error` stays silent because its diagnostic already exists at the parser. Pinned at unit level (the arm is unreachable through source), with the old panic planted to prove the pin reddens. (backlog B144)

## v0.37.0 — 2026-08-27

> **Upgrade if you are on v0.36.0 or earlier: this release fixes two
> miscompiles that were live in every released toolchain, and both were
> silent.** Neither produced a diagnostic, because neither was a mistake
> about the program — the types, the inference and the call graph were all
> right, and only the emitted JavaScript was wrong, which is the one class a
> type checker cannot see.
>
> - **A field or method read directly off an awaited call read the promise
>   instead of the value**, and evaluated to `undefined`. `read_bytes(path).len()`
>   compiled clean and exited 0. This is exactly the spelling the async model
>   invites — a call to an `async` function is supposed to feel synchronous —
>   so the more idiomatic the code, the more likely it was wrong. Binding
>   first (`let bytes = read_bytes(path); bytes.len()`) was always correct,
>   which is why `std` and the corpus never tripped it. (B141)
> - **An `is` test in a loop condition did not re-read its subject**, so a
>   body reassignment never reached the test: a loop ran every iteration where
>   one was correct, and without a bounding conjunct it did not terminate at
>   all. (B136)
>
> If you have code matching either shape, re-run it rather than re-reading it.


<!-- family: breaking -->
**`asset::emit`'s kind must name one file — a kind carrying `..` or a separator is refused at compile time instead of steering the build's write out of `dist/`.** The kind is not a label, it is a filename: the pairs `asset::emit` accumulates during const evaluation are assembled per kind and written beside the compiled JavaScript as `output_js.with_extension(kind)`. Nothing checked it. A kind of `"../evil"` therefore resolved to a path outside the output directory and the build wrote there, and a kind of `"a/b"` named a file in a directory the build never made. The sibling in the same channel had the fence all along — `asset::read` refuses an absolute path and refuses any component that is not `Normal`/`CurDir`, lexically and before any filesystem look, so a read cannot climb out of the package root. The input direction was fenced and the output direction was not, which is the whole of the defect. `check_emit_kind` now applies the same shape to the write direction: one rule, taken on the value at const time where the kind is finally known, refusing anything that is not a single path segment — the two shapes an audit found (`../evil` and `a/b`) are the same mistake, since a kind names ONE file. The rule is structural rather than a charset allowlist, so it refuses every path a kind could become while leaving every kind a real `emit` passes untouched, and the refusal names the fix. **What changes for you:** a project that passed a kind containing `/`, `\`, `..` or `.` stops compiling and must pass a bare kind like `css`; a kind with a separator was already broken in practice, since the directory it named did not exist and the write failed at the filesystem, so the refusal mostly converts a runtime write error into a diagnostic that says what is wrong. Pinned in both directions, including a green negative asserting an ordinary kind is untouched — a fence that refused a legitimate kind would be worse than the hole it closes. (backlog E94)

The neighbouring question was asked and answered the other way, which is worth stating so it is not re-filed as an oversight: a manifest dependency `path` gets no `..` containment check, on purpose. `[entry.<name>] path` does refuse `..`, and the asymmetry is the decision. An entry path names a file inside the package being built — resolved against `root`, its name becoming `dist/<name>.js` — so climbing out is meaningless there and the guard is well-formedness, not a security boundary. A dependency path names another package, and pointing outside the declaring directory is the entire point of the form: `common = { path = "../common" }` is how every workspace spells it, this repository's own `examples/fullstack` legs included, so a guard there would refuse the correct spelling of the common case. The security argument for adding one anyway does not survive the ruled trust model (`proposal/build-trust.md` §2): the manifest is the developer's own file, and building a project already runs its `[build] run` hooks with the developer's full privileges, no sandbox and no prompt — so against a manifest free to execute arbitrary shell, a rule about which directory it may name buys nothing and advertises a containment property the ruling explicitly declines to offer. The decision is now written where the code is, at `DependencySource::Path`, and pinned so it cannot be quietly converted into the entry rule by a later reader who sees only the asymmetry. (backlog E95)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`std::fs`'s synchronous read is gone — `read_file_to_str_sync` and `read_file_bytes_sync` are removed, because the justification recorded at them was false.** The doc comment said the sync read existed "for a read that must complete inside a callback that cannot suspend", and named the caller: "a synchronous request handler has no other option, and `serve_build`'s dev-mode revalidation is exactly that". No such caller existed, and none existed when the sentence was written. The request path is async end to end and was already: `Server.request_handler` is typed `async |Request| Response`, `ServerBuilder`'s field likewise (its own comment reads "the handler may suspend, and `start()` awaits its response before writing"), `on_request` takes an async handler, `respond_from_build` is async because it awaits the app's fallback, and the `node:http` callback suspends on `read_request_bytes` before it writes a single byte. The handler channel went async on 2026-07-13; the sync read arrived on 2026-08-11, four weeks later, justified by a constraint that had already been removed. The one path that could once have supported the claim — a second, genuinely synchronous handler (`serve_service`/`build_handler`) — was deleted in v0.35.0. In the same file as the sync read, `build_of` and `require_build` await their reads on the same boot path, and `require_shell` does an async boot read out of the same `main`.

So the two reads are deleted rather than re-explained, and their callers take the async read: `load_build` (boot) and `asset_body` (the per-request revalidation the false comment named) become async by inference, and `ServerBuilder::serve_build` with them. Nothing at a call site changes shape — an async call in vilan is implicitly awaited, so `serve_build(build)` still reads as one link in the builder chain and the whole dev policy still holds in both directions: an edit to `dist/` is served without a restart under `run --watch`, and the boot-time copy is served outside one. That is the point being made. **A sync variant of an async operation has to name the caller that cannot suspend**; if it cannot name one, the async call already feels synchronous to its caller and the variant is only a way to block the event loop. Upgrading is deleting the suffix: `read_file_to_str_sync(p)` becomes `read_file_to_str(p)`, and the enclosing function becomes async the way every other reader in this library already is. `read_file_bytes_sync` needed removing on a second count as well — it returned `str`, not `Bytes`, the exact name-lie F13 fixed on the async side on 2026-08-11 by renaming `read_file_bytes` to `read_file_encoded`; the sync twin kept the discarded name.

A sweep of the rest of the standard library for the same defect found the remaining synchronous bindings honest but two of them silent, so they now say why they exist. **`fs::exists` stays synchronous** and its comment no longer implies a caller that cannot suspend, because it does not have one: `build_of`, `load_build` and the todo example's `load` are all async around it. It is synchronous because `node:fs/promises` has no `exists` at all, so `existsSync` is the whole operation in one call, and because syncness is the only thing separating it from `stat(path).is_some()` — which is in the same module, is async, and is what to reach for on a request path. Its cost is one blocking stat, which is what node implements `existsSync` as: fine for a boot-time branch, not for a per-request one. **`rpc_server::create_hash`** is the one place in std where the usual claim is true, and it had no comment at all: the WebSocket handshake runs inside `upgrade_handler`, typed `|NodeRequest, NodeSocket, Bytes| void` — a plain closure, not an `async` one — so it genuinely cannot suspend, which is why that binding diverges from `std::crypto`'s "async where the host is" policy. Recorded at the function, along with the condition that would make it worth revisiting. (`node:sqlite` exposes only `DatabaseSync`, so `std::db` has no choice to document, and `Drop` must be synchronous by construction.) Docs `std/process.md` and `guide/persistence.md`.

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`serve_build` carries bytes end to end — a favicon, a font or a wasm module now reaches the wire exactly as the build wrote it.** The pipeline was text-typed at every point: `BuildAsset.content` was a `str` and `asset_body` returned one, so every artifact was UTF-8-decoded on the way in and re-encoded on the way out. For the artifacts `serve_build` could type — javascript, css, json, html — that round trip was lossless and invisible. For anything else it was destruction, and it was the reason nothing else could be typed at all: a content type naming `image/png` over a decoded body would have been a promise the pipeline had already broken. kolt measured it on its own favicon before hand-rolling a static layer to get around it — 483 bytes on disk, 853 bytes served, because every byte that is not a legal UTF-8 sequence becomes a three-byte U+FFFD. `BuildAsset.content` is now `Bytes`, `asset_body` returns `Bytes`, and `asset_response` answers with `body_bytes`, so the bytes the build wrote are the bytes on the wire. The boot read is `fs::read_bytes`, and no synchronous variant was added to reach it: the whole path is async and the standing rule is that a sync variant must name the caller that cannot suspend. Both halves of the dev-mode freshness policy read the same way they store — a `run --watch` re-read that decoded would have served a freshly corrupted copy of every binary artifact, differing from the boot copy in exactly the bytes a PNG is made of. **What changes for you:** reading `BuildAsset.content` or `asset_body`'s result as text becomes `decode_utf8(...)`; `serve_build` itself is unchanged at every call site. Docs `std/process.md`, `guide/persistence.md`. (kolt.local 030)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`str::substring` refuses an out-of-range slice instead of correcting it; `strip_prefix`/`strip_suffix` ship alongside.** `substring` was the raw host method, so it inherited JavaScript's two silent corrections: a negative bound clamps to `0`, and `start > end` **swaps** the pair. Together those meant `s.substring(offset, -1)` — the obvious way to write "cut the last character" — returned `s[0..offset]`, the **prefix**: not an error, not a truncation, but the exact complement of the string asked for, at compile time and at run time alike, with the docs saying only "end-exclusive". The rule is now one inequality, `0 <= start <= end <= len`, and everything else is refused. No clamping, no swapping, and `end > len` is refused too rather than truncated — a caller who means "to the end" writes `s.len()`. Note plainly: calls that are legal today stop working on upgrade — with **literal** bounds they stop compiling (`substring end -1 is negative — the range must satisfy 0 <= start <= end <= len, and substring never clamps or swaps`, spanned on the offending argument, with a note naming the replacement verb), and with computed bounds they **panic** (`substring out of range: the length is 12 but the range is 7..-1 — …`). The degenerate ranges inside the rule stay legal: `substring(0, 0)`, `substring(len, len)`, `substring(0, len)`. Const evaluation moved in lockstep — the interpreter arm reproduced JS's clamp-and-swap faithfully, which would have folded a wrong string into a build rather than failing it, and it now refuses in the same words the runtime prints.

This is the refusal doctrine this tree applies everywhere else, finally applied here: the cut script refuses an entry with no family marker rather than guessing, `serve_build` leaves an unknown extension unserved rather than guessed at, the manifest refuses a reserved name instead of resolving it cleverly. A silent reinterpretation is the guess-instead-of-refuse those all reject — and worse than a guess, because it answers with the complement. Rust makes the call unspellable (`&s[a..b]` takes `usize`) and offers `strip_prefix`/`strip_suffix` returning `Option<&str>` for what people actually want; vilan now refuses, and ships the same two verbs (in `std::option`, beside `parse_i32`, since the always-loaded `str` core must stay off the `Option` chain). They return `Option<str>` so that "absent" and "present but empty" stay distinguishable: `"ab".strip_prefix("ab")` is `Some("")`, `"ab".strip_prefix("zz")` is `None`. `starts_with`/`ends_with` test; these two cut. No range verb was added — `substring` already is one, and a second spelling of the same operation would be two names for one thing; `Bytes::slice` exists because `Bytes` had none.

Auditing the tree against the new rule found **five latent bugs**, all fixed here, all previously masked by the very clamping that is now gone: `document.vl`'s markup scanner probed `substring(index, index + 4)` for `<!--` with no room check (any `<` in the last three bytes) and read an attribute's `=` and quote at an index `skip_spaces` had already run to the end of (any unterminated tag), and both RPC frame routers computed a reply slice from `split(":")[0]` that inverts when the frame carries no second colon — remotely reachable on the server side, where a client sending `r:7` produced an inverted range. Each is now a length check or an explicit ignore, matching the routers' stated forward-compatibility contract. Emitted output changes shape: `s.substring(a, b)` lowers to the checked `__substring(s, a, b)` helper, the way `list[i]` already lowers to `__at` for the same reason; two corpus goldens move accordingly. Docs `std/strings.md` (which documented no out-of-range behavior at all — part of the bug) and the errors appendix. (kolt.local tracker item 023, RULED 2026-08-26.)

---

<!-- family: breaking -->
**`vilan` joins the reserved package names; the manifest now refuses it.** L12 reserved the three import roots (`std`, `pkg`, `macro_std`) — the names the toolchain itself owns — but `vilan`, the language's own name, was not among them: a `[package]` could name itself `vilan` and a dependency key could claim it, staking out the name the official-package namespace decision may need (the owner's recorded position on the std/auxiliary split: bundle a `vilan::` namespace with releases until a registry is up — a re-ruling still to come). Reserving it is the cheap first step either way, and cheapest before any user package claims it. The refusal is L12's, at the same layer: `` `vilan` is a reserved package name: the language owns its own name (`std`, `pkg`, `macro_std`, and `vilan` are all reserved); rename the package `` — for `[package] name` and dependency keys in `[package.dependencies]`, `[library.dependencies]`, and `[project.dependencies]`, the CLI refusing before any dependency work and the editor publishing on the `vilan.toml`; `[library] name` stays exempt, and the sibling refusals' wording moves with the set (the parenthetical now names four). Note plainly: a manifest that names a package or a dependency `vilan` was legal yesterday and stops compiling on upgrade — the rename is the whole fix. Spec §4.2/§11.4, tour "Projects and dependencies"; the reservation is kolt.local tracker item 026's recorded first step (owner's position, 2026-08-26), riding L12's mechanism (`proposals/proposal/std-shape.md` §4).

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**A field or method read directly off an awaited call read the promise, not the value.** `read_bytes(path).len()` compiled clean, exited 0, and evaluated to `undefined`. The emitter rendered an await as `await (<operand>)` — parenthesising the OPERAND, never the whole await-expression — and then rendered a `.field`, a `[index]` and a call's callee with no parens of their own. Member access and call are JavaScript's tightest-binding forms, so `await (f()).x` parses as `await ((f()).x)`: the property was read off the **promise**, and awaiting `undefined` gives `undefined`. No diagnostic fired, because nothing was wrong with the program — the type system, the inference and the call graph all had it right, and only the printing was wrong, which is the kind of defect a type checker cannot see. This was live in every released toolchain, and it is exactly the spelling the async model invites: the stated design is that calling an `async` function should feel synchronous to the caller, and it does, everywhere except the emitter. Binding first (`let bytes = read_bytes(path); bytes.len()`) was always correct, which is why neither `std` nor the corpus — which bind — ever tripped it.

Four shapes were wrong. A **field** off an awaited call (`fetch_row().id`) and a **built-in method** (`fetch_list().len()`, which lowers to `.length`, so it is the field shape again) both went silently `undefined`. A **postfix chain** (`fetch_row().name.len()`) threw `Cannot read properties of undefined`, the first link having already taken the promise. **Calling a callable an async function returned** (`make()()`) threw `is not a function` — that one never went silent. And the bug was not confined to the implicit await: a hand-written `(await pending).id` emitted `await (pending)[0]` and read `undefined` too, the author's own parentheses dropped and then not reprinted. Two neighbouring shapes were already correct, but only by accident, both because the await happened to land in argument position where it is parenthesised for free: a **subscript** goes through the checked `__at` helper, and a **user-defined method** lowers to a free function call. Both are now pinned, so neither can regress silently if either lowering ever changes.

The fix is a general rule, not a wrap at the three broken sites. The printer already had one half of the precedence question answered — an `operand` helper that decides whether a child survives unwrapped in BINARY-OPERAND position, where the hazard is a child binding too loosely. It had no counterpart for POSTFIX-SUBJECT position, and the one case anybody had hit (`(() => …)()`, a directly-called closure) sat open-coded in the call arm as a lone `matches!`. That open-coded case was this helper, one node wide. There is now a `postfix_subject` beside `operand`, applied by all three postfix arms, wrapping any subject that binds looser than a postfix does — an await, a unary, a binary, an assignment, a closure. The alternative was to emit `(await x)` unconditionally at every site; it was rejected because it is a producer-side defensive wrap in a printer that decides parentheses at the consumer, it would leave the actual defect (postfix subjects rendered with no regard for precedence at all) standing for every other loose-binding node, and it moves output for no semantic gain — measured at 88 lines across 7 corpus goldens, every one of them a cosmetic paren. The context-aware rule moves **zero** goldens: the emitted bytes for all 114 corpus programs are unchanged, which is the same fact as "the corpus always binds first" seen from the other side. Ten pins land with the fix, one per shape including the two accidental-correctness cases and a bound-spelling control, each of them a pin that RUNS the emitted program and asserts the value — the miscompile is invisible to a compile-only assertion, which is how it reached release. A corpus program (`await-postfix.vl`) makes the parentheses a byte-level gate as well. (backlog B141)

---

<!-- family: miscompile -->
**An `is` test in a loop condition now reads its subject each iteration.** The transformer hoisted the condition's subject temp (`const $a = subject;`) into the enclosing block, before the `while` — one evaluation for the whole loop — so a body reassignment never reached the test: `for (found is None) && cursor < 3 { found = Some(cursor); … }` ran all three iterations where one is correct, and without the bounding conjunct the loop never ended, which is how it surfaced (the markdown spike port hung). `vilan check` was clean; the defect was codegen, live in released toolchains through v0.36.0. A loop condition that needs statements — an `is` subject temp and its materialized captures above all — now compiles to `while (true) { <prelude> if (!cond) break; <body> }`: the prelude re-runs per evaluation, each evaluation still reads its subject exactly once, and `jump continue` re-enters at the prelude; a statement-free condition emits byte-identically to before, and `if` position was never affected. `std::markdown`'s `parse_inline` drops the bool-flag workaround it shipped with. (`proposal/markdown.md` §10.7's finding, B136; pinned per loop form — the repro, the unbounded and `jump break` forms, nested loops, a condition binding, two tests in one condition, and a `Result` variant, beside the `if`-position control and a release-preset twin.)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**A built app needs nothing but `dist/`: `const asset::bundle` carries a non-code resource into the build and gives you back the url its copy answers on.** Until now a program that depended on a resource read that resource out of its own source tree at run time — kolt's HTTP server read `src/static/*` and `src/head.html`, and this project's own website hand-copies `playground/editor.js` out of `playground/` in CI with a `cp` line, because `vilan build` would not carry it. Both are the same defect wearing two costumes: a deployed `dist/` was not a deployable app. `let icon = const asset::bundle("static/icon.svg");` writes `dist/static/icon.svg`, folds to `"/static/icon.svg"`, and puts the file in the leg's build manifest — so `serve_build` serves it with no route of your own, typed by the generated mime table. The gate is literal: a test builds a two-leg project, **removes the source tree**, then runs `dist/server.mjs` and fetches the icon.

It is the third direction of a channel that already had two, and it is deliberately the sibling of `read` rather than of `emit`. `emit` accumulates *lines* into one generated file, deduplicating and sorting them — a shape no `.png` survives, and one that gives every kind exactly one output name. So `bundle` reuses what `read` established instead: package-root-relative resolution, a lexical refusal of anything absolute or escaping, const-only under the same reachability fixpoint (a function reaching it is compile-time-only and has no runtime value form), and a tracked build input, which is what makes `run --watch` recopy an edited resource with no `.vl` file touched. It diverges on fuel — `read` charges per byte because its bytes become a `str` the const program computes over, while a bundled file's bytes never enter the program at all, so a size charge would have capped how large a resource may be rather than how much work a build does.

The path IS the name, and that settles three questions at once. A subdirectory survives, so `playground/editor.js` stays under `/playground/` rather than being flattened onto the site root by a basename rule; two different files can never claim one output name, so there is no collision policy to get wrong; and nothing is renamed behind the author's back, so where a resource lands in `dist/` is decided by where the file is put. Names are `/`-separated on every host and a backslash is refused rather than translated, for the reason `std::path` is POSIX-shaped — the name is derived *output*, and a separator-aware rule would make every url, manifest row and golden host-dependent. Reachability stays the compiler's: a resource sitting beside a bundled one, named by no `const`, is never copied and does not ship. That is the whole difference between this and a copy step, and the reason 029 chose an import-file function over a build script.

Three refusals guard the output directory, and one of them is not about collision at all. A name a leg's own build owns — `client.js`, `client.css`, `client.chunks.json`, or anything in the `client.<arm>.js` chunk namespace, for *every* leg, since `dist/` is one directory — fails the build, because the two sweeps that keep a manifest honest would otherwise **delete** a resource parked there on the next build rather than merely overwrite it this one. A source that is already its own destination is left alone: a bare file bundling a sibling resolves both to one path, and `fs::copy` over itself truncates, so the naive copy would destroy the very file it was asked to carry. And a bundled file that is not on disk is an error at the `const` expression, recorded as an input anyway so its appearance invalidates the compile that failed on it.

Alongside it, `std::build` moved its own path arithmetic onto `std::path`: `artifacts()` joins with `path::join` instead of `i"{dist}/{name}"`, and `content_type_of` reads the extension through `path::extname` instead of `file.split(".").last()` — a hand-rolled `extname` carrying the classic dotfile bug, which typed `dist/.css` as a stylesheet. That stopped being hypothetical the moment a bundled resource could live in a subdirectory. (kolt.local 029, 017; see-also 022, 024, 027, 030)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
