**`std::fs` grows the writes and the directory surface it never had — starting with `write_bytes`, without which bytes could come in and never go out.** `read_bytes` landed in v0.34.0 and `writeFile` stayed bound exactly once, typed `(path: str, contents: str)`, so a vilan program could read a favicon, a font or a wasm module and had no way to write one back. It was a one-line binding nobody had written: the host's `writeFile` accepts a `Uint8Array` directly and `Bytes` is one, so `write_bytes` is the same host function bound at its other argument type — exactly how `read_bytes` and `read_file_encoded` already share `readFile`. The pin asserts byte-identity rather than success, over a payload a text round trip destroys (the PNG magic number followed by all 256 byte values), because success is not the claim: the 483-byte favicon that arrived on the wire as 853 bytes is what a decode-and-re-encode does to a binary file, and this is the write that makes it unrepresentable.

Eleven more functions arrive with it, all asynchronous, none with a synchronous twin. On the write side: `write_bytes_atomic`, the byte twin of `write_atomic` (same uniquely-named sibling, same rename, so an image a running server is reading is replaced whole or not at all); `append`, which extends and never truncates and creates a missing file — the right call for a log and the wrong one for a store, since an append is not atomic; `copy`, where the source survives, which is the whole difference from `rename`; `remove`, which deletes a file and refuses a directory, keeping the host's split rather than papering over it; and `update(path, |str| str)`, the read-modify-write a JSON store or a rewritten config is made of, with the write half already atomic. `update` is not a lock and its comment says so: two processes calling it on one path can still interleave read-read-write-write, and the second write wins whole. It closes the *tearing* window, not the *racing* one, and closing that would need advisory locking the host cannot reach at all. Its `revise` is a plain value-returning closure, so it is asyncness-polymorphic — a revision that awaits makes that `update` await too, with nothing written at either the declaration or the call site.

On the directory side the forms come in strict-and-forgiving pairs, deliberately. `create_dir` makes one level and fails with `EEXIST` if something is already there, which makes it the module's only exclusive-create primitive until a file handle's `create_new` exists; `create_dir_all` makes every missing level and is idempotent, because "make sure this place exists before I write into it" has no business failing for having already run. `remove_dir` refuses a directory that is not empty; `remove_dir_all` takes the tree and everything under it. `copy_dir` copies a tree into its destination, creating it if needed, overwriting files that have a counterpart in the source and leaving files that do not — a merge, not a mirror. `remove_dir_all` is the only call besides `stat` in this module that does not throw on a missing path, and that exception is reasoned rather than inherited: "make sure this is gone" is already satisfied by a path that was never there, which is `stat`'s own logic applied to a destructive call instead of a probing one. Every other failure of it still throws, and the module header now names both exceptions in one place.

`scan_dir(path)` is `read_dir` carrying the kinds the host had all along and `read_dir` threw away. `readdir` under `withFileTypes` returns the kind with the name, so telling a file from a directory stops costing a `stat` per entry — a thousand extra syscalls on a thousand-entry directory, which is precisely what `read_dir`'s own doc comment used to send callers off to do. Each entry is an `Entry { name, is_directory, is_file, is_symlink }`: three booleans rather than a kind enum, because a host directory entry has *nine* kinds — file, directory, symlink, FIFO, socket, block device, character device, unknown — and an enum would have to model five nobody will ever meet or carry a catch-all meaning "one of five things I did not model". An entry that is none of the three reads back with all three `false`, which is a true statement about it rather than a wrong one, and is pinned that way against a real unix socket. The kinds do not follow symlinks, on purpose: a link to a directory is `is_symlink = true` with `is_directory = false`, which is what lets a recursive walker see a loop before it walks into one. `stat` does follow, and remains how you ask what a link points at.

One mechanism note is worth recording, because the design paper this slice comes from predicted the opposite: an options object needs no host glue. `read_dir_all` reaches into `transformer.rs` for a `__fs_read_dir_all` helper to spell its single `{ recursive: true }`, and `proposal/filesystem.md` §3.1 carried that forward as a seam every options-taking function would have to ride. It is not one. `[extern("Object")]` mints a fresh `{}` and `[extern(set, …)]` fills it in, both forms already existed, and `std::fetch` has been building `RequestInit` exactly this way the whole time. So `create_dir_all`, `remove_dir_all`, `copy_dir` and `scan_dir` are plain vilan over direct bindings, the compiler is untouched, and `__fs_read_dir_all` is now the odd one out rather than the precedent. What is still missing is the part that needs a ruling and not a binding: an open file handle, and with it incremental reads, positional writes and `fsync` — the only route to durability rather than mere atomicity — plus a file watch. Docs `std/process.md`. (kolt.local 031, `proposal/filesystem.md` §3.1, slice S1)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Request::header(name)` — request headers are readable at last, so a conditional request can be answered in user land.** `std::http`'s `Request` exposed `path`, `method`, `body` and `bytes` and no header accessor at all, which put every request-side header — `If-None-Match`, `If-Modified-Since`, `Accept`, `Authorization` — outside the supported surface. The raw headers did exist one layer down, but only by reaching through `Request`'s private-ish `node` field to `NodeRequest::headers()` and decoding the result with `JsonValue::field` plus `json::coerce_str`; kolt's `get_cache_control` records the surrender in a comment and re-sends every asset body on every revalidation because of it. The accessor returns `Option<str>`, because a header may legitimately be absent and `None` has to stay distinct from `Some("")` for one sent empty — precisely the distinction a validator check turns on.

Case-insensitivity is handled, not assumed: Header names are case-insensitive, and node lowercases every name it parses off the wire, so `header` lowers the name you pass to match: the canonical `header("If-None-Match")` finds a header the client sent as `IF-NONE-MATCH`, which the naive spelling through `field()` silently does not. Presence is `Object.hasOwn` rather than a null test, for two reasons found by the pin rather than by reading: an absent header is a *missing* property, which `JsonValue::is_null`'s strict `=== null` does not catch (it returned the string `"undefined"`), and hasOwn also stops an inherited name like `header("constructor")` from reading back as a header. Both are pinned, alongside present and case-mismatched, by a test that speaks raw HTTP to the server from outside the process — the only way to pin claims about what the wire actually carried.

Repeated headers are documented rather than papered over: A header the client repeated reads back as node presents it, joined with `", "` — the list form ordinary headers already define — so nothing is silently dropped, and nothing is split for you either. `set-cookie` is node's one unjoined header, kept as an array, and it arrives here comma-joined *without* the space; since a cookie value may itself contain a comma that string is not safely splittable, so repeated `set-cookie` is explicitly not readable through this accessor and a list-returning one is left as future work (`set-cookie` is a response header, so on a request it is already degenerate). This is ask (a) of the ETag item and stands alone: it unblocks user-land conditional requests today. A caching policy for `serve_build` (b) and an ETag/304 helper (c) are still open, (b) on a fenced design question that is the owner's call. Docs `std/process.md`. (kolt.local 025 ask a)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::crypto::sha256` / `sha384` / `sha512` — content digests, at last.** std had no unkeyed digest anywhere, and the two things that looked like one were not: `std::hash` is the Map/Set canonical-key mechanism, which aggregates a key by its JSON string and promises no avalanche at all, and `std::crypto` carried only the keyed and derived primitives (`hmac_sha512`, `pbkdf2_sha512`). `crypto.subtle.digest` was simply never bound. The gap's exhibit is kolt, whose asset fingerprints are computed **out of band** — nothing in the language can produce them — so `src/server.vl`'s `is_fingerprinted` can only *recognize* a hash shape after the fact, walking eight characters through `code_at` against raw ASCII ranges to decide whether a name carries one. The three functions take `Bytes` and return `Bytes`, pairing with the existing `Bytes::to_hex()` for the hex form a fingerprint or a cache validator is actually written as; `sha256(bytes).to_hex().substring(0, 8)` is the eight-hex-digit name kolt had to mint outside the language. Pinned against the published FIPS 180-4 vectors for all three widths rather than against this implementation's own output, plus the empty-input edge.

Async, with no sync twin, deliberately: These follow WebCrypto, which is async, and that is the standing `std::crypto` stance (`std/misc.md`): a path that genuinely cannot suspend binds the host's sync primitive as an extern instead — the way the walkthrough binds Node's `pbkdf2Sync` — and no caller here needs that. A sync variant would have to name the caller that cannot suspend, and there isn't one. Note what these are *not* for: a raw digest is far too fast to hash a password with, and `pbkdf2_sha512` remains the primitive for that. Docs `std/misc.md`. (kolt.local 024)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::fs` can now replace a file without ever tearing it — `write_atomic`, over a new `rename` binding.** `write_file` truncates its target and then fills it, so a process that dies partway through leaves a half-written file. That is not a theoretical hazard: the `todo` example rewrites its whole JSON store on every change and reads it back at boot, treating a decode failure as an empty list — so a crash mid-write produced torn JSON, the next boot read it as `[]`, and the store's own subscription immediately persisted that `[]` over the only surviving copy. The whole list, gone, with a clean exit code. `write_atomic(path, contents)` writes to a uniquely-named sibling and renames it over the target instead, so a crash leaves either the previous file intact or the complete new one; the example now uses it. `rename(from, to)` is the primitive underneath and is also the move/replace operation `std::fs` has never had — the toolchain's own Rust has done temp-sibling-plus-rename in three subsystems all along, while vilan code had no way to express it. Atomic is not durable: closing the power-loss gap needs `fsync`, which the host exposes only on an open file handle, and std has no handle type yet. (kolt.local item 031; `proposal/filesystem.md` designs the rest of that surface.)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`serve_build`'s content-type table is generated from `mime-db` instead of hand-written at four rows.** The table covered `.js`/`.mjs`, `.css`, `.json` and `.html`, and an artifact with any other extension was skipped — so a build that emitted a `.png`, `.svg`, `.woff2`, `.ico`, `.webmanifest` or `.wasm` had those artifacts silently unserved, and kolt moved its fonts, icons and manifest to a hand-rolled static layer with a twenty-arm mime table of its own. The fence that kept the table small was recorded as a design decision and it still stands — `serve_build` serves a build, not a directory — but its *stated reason* had gone stale: it argued that no vilan program could serve a PNG at all, which stopped being true when `read_bytes` landed and is emphatically untrue now. The reason is rewritten to the one that was always doing the work, which is scope, and the table grows to match what a build actually emits. **Where the rows come from matters more than how many there are.** Nothing is typed from memory: the rows are derived from [`mime-db`](https://github.com/jshttp/mime-db) — the IANA/Apache/nginx registry aggregate that vite's own `mrmime` is generated from — into a checked-in dataset carrying each row's provenance, and the vilan `match` arms are generated from that dataset and byte-gated on every suite run (`crates/vilan-core/tests/mime_table_sync.rs`, regenerated with `VILAN_REGENERATE_MIME_TABLE=1`; the dataset is refreshed by `scripts/regen-mime-table.py`). Exactly one row overrules the dataset and says so: `.ico` is served `image/x-icon` rather than the registered `image/vnd.microsoft.icon`, because the latter is what nothing sends — `mrmime` resolves the same conflict by dropping `.ico` entirely, which would fail the favicon that motivated this. Three details the gate holds in both directions. A `text/*` row spells `; charset=utf-8`, because the body goes out as raw bytes and so carries no encoding of its own — an unspelled `text/css` is decoded by whatever the browser defaults to. `application/json` and its `+json` relatives take **no** charset, being utf8 by spec, where naming one is the error rather than the fix. And `.webmanifest` is `application/manifest+json`, because Chrome rejects a manifest served as anything else. Audio and video are deliberately absent, for a reason worth stating positively: `serve_build` writes a whole body and honours no `Range` header, so a browser could not seek in anything it served — a row for `.mp4` would type a response that does not work. An extension the table does not name is still not served rather than guessed at, and the skip is still said rather than silent. Docs `std/process.md`, `guide/persistence.md`. (kolt.local 022)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`i32::max_value()` / `i32::min_value()` — the integer bounds, finally askable.** The ranges were documented in prose only, and arithmetic past a type's range is undefined behavior — which makes the boundary values load-bearing and, until now, unaskable: the sole spelling was hardcoding `2147483647`. All eight integer types gain the pair — `i8`, `u8`, `i16`, `u16`, `i32`, `u32`, `i53`, `u53` — with `0` for the unsigned minimums and the symmetric ±2^53 window for the wide pair. **A deliberate stopgap.** vilan has no associated-const or static-member mechanism for `i32::MAX` to hang on, so rather than wait for that design the bounds ship as niladic functions; when it lands they become `i32::MAX`/`i32::MIN` and this pair enters a `[deprecated("steer")]` window that rewrites callers, making the rename scheduled rather than a surprise — the intent is recorded at the functions themselves, where a reader meets them. The pair reports the **type's** range, which is deliberately not the compiler's literal-admission range: the signed check tests the magnitude so `-128i8` can be written at all, so `128i8` compiles while `i8::max_value()` is `127`. That gap is pinned, as is the agreement between each bound and the analyzer's own out-of-range diagnostic, so the table is cross-checked rather than transcribed. Floats get no pair, for stated reasons: `f64`'s finite bounds cannot be written as vilan literals at all (there is no exponent syntax), and `min_value()` would have to silently prejudge the most-negative-finite versus smallest-positive-normal split that the eventual `f64::MIN` should settle. Docs `std/numbers.md`. (kolt dogfood, item 021)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Style.size(value)` — width and height in one call.** The sizing pair was the one multi-property group in `std::style` without an axis shorthand — `padding_x`/`padding_y`, `margin_x`/`margin_y`, and `inset` all have one — and the square case is the measured demand: an icon box spells `width(Length::rem(1.0)).height(Length::rem(1.0))` today. One arity only, deliberately: `size` writes the same two slots `width` and `height` write, so a later `height` narrows the square under the ordinary last-wins rule, and the rectangle keeps its `width(w).height(h)` spelling — the no-multi-value-shorthand rule of `proposal/ui-styling.md` §0bis.5. Docs `std/style.md`. (kolt dogfood, item 011)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Color::var(name)` — the typed spelling of a CSS-variable-backed colour.** `Length::var` has been the typed end of the dynamic-value channel since the core shipped; `Color` had no counterpart, so a variable-backed colour was spelled through `Color::hex("var(--x)")` — an undocumented abuse of an escape hatch named for hex literals — or by reaching past the constructor API into the struct literal. Same semantics as `Length::var`: the reference renders `var(--name)` and **declares nothing** — the app owns the custom-property declaration (its emitted theme block, or `view.style_var` writing it at runtime) — and `.alpha()` composes over it through the relative-colour form exactly as over a ramp token. Docs `std/style.md`. (kolt dogfood, item 012)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Color::oklch(lightness, chroma, hue)` — the perceptual colour literal.** "oklch" had zero hits in the whole tree while the design language derives its palette by hand along stated hue angles — exactly the workflow the space exists to serve: hold a hue, step the lightness, and the steps look even across hues, which rgb cannot promise. The CSS **number** form: lightness 0.0–1.0 (not a percentage), chroma 0.0–0.5 (0 is achromatic; sRGB tops out near 0.37), hue in degrees in its canonical 0–360 turn — angles wrap in CSS, and a class name is a content hash, so admitting `700` beside `340` would mint two classes for one colour. All three ranges are validated during const evaluation like `rgba`'s channels, and `.alpha()` composes over the result through the relative-colour form. `oklab` is deliberately not shipped alongside: its signed a/b axes are a different validation and a different authoring model, and the palette workflow asks for the polar form. Docs `std/style.md`. (kolt dogfood, item 013)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`Style::attribute(name, value, inner)` — selecting on `data-*`/`aria-*` state, a fourth condition axis.** The machinery existed hardcoded for exactly one case — `dark()` emits `:root[data-theme="dark"] .sX` — so state carried in markup (`data-open`, `data-state`, `aria-expanded`) had no styled spelling and apps emitted those rules by hand through `asset::emit`. `attribute` is the generalization, with two deliberate differences from `dark`: it conditions on the element **itself** (`.sX[data-open="true"]` — dark's ancestor stays the one ancestor form), and it takes its **own slot** in the condition axis, between dark and the pseudo-class — `md(dark(attribute(.., hover(..))))` — so a dark theme still composes with an element's state, which a shared slot would have refused. The slot key carries the attribute condition, so last-wins merge stays per-(condition, property): two values of one attribute are two conditions and coexist, the same value overrides. Every wrong nesting is a const-eval refusal naming the fix, mirroring dark's; names and values refuse quotes, spaces and `:` at const time (they delimit the slot key, the condition grammar, and the selector's own quoting). Every pre-existing class name is byte-identical — the condition grammar grew a token, not a field. `dark()` itself is untouched; whether it becomes sugar for one attribute condition is item 014's question, now sharpened. Docs `std/style.md`. (kolt dogfood, item 010)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`const` code can read project files: `std::asset::read`, and a fuel budget sized to real workloads.** The compile-time asset channel gains its input direction — `asset::read(path)` returns a project file's text during const evaluation, so `const markdown::parse(asset::read("pages/intro.md"))` parses a page at build time and ships the AST as plain data (the docs-port prerequisite `proposal/docs-port.md` §3.3 filed, step 2 of K13). Paths resolve against the **package root** — the base imports resolve under, never the process working directory — and an absolute path or one escaping the root is refused before any read. Every file read is a **tracked build input**: `--watch` re-runs when one changes or a previously missing one appears, an unchanged-source watch round still recompiles a leg whose read inputs changed, and a missing file is a clean compile error at the `const` expression naming the path and where resolution landed. `read` is compile-time-only under exactly the machinery that colors `emit` — a runtime call path is refused at the boundary, a value escape at the reference. The explicit-const fuel budget rises 1M → **16M**, sized by measurement rather than estimate: parsing the book's largest page (`docs/spec/memory.md`, 40,758 bytes) under const evaluation costs 2,001,457 fuel — the old budget could not even char-scan it (`docs-port.md` §2.1) — so 16M carries 8× headroom while a runaway `const` still fails in about a second (reads charge fuel per byte, so the budget bounds input size too; the budget-miss diagnostic and its remedy are unchanged, and inferred-const budgets deliberately do not move). `VILAN_PHASE_TIMING` now prints `const-fuel-max`, the instrument the sizing came from. Spec §9.2–§9.3, `std::asset` in the std reference, and the errors appendix carry the new surface.

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::process::cwd()` — the working directory, readable at last.** `std::process` was exit/scan/args/env: std itself leaned on a working directory nothing in the language could read — `build_of` hardcodes `"dist"` because "a server runs from the project root", and `BuildError::NotBuilt`'s message blames a wrong working directory — while the only near-workaround was `env("PWD")`, shell-dependent and absent outright when the process is not started from a POSIX shell (the dogfood exhibit: a server that panicked at boot unless a hand-set `CWD` env var said where it was). One extern to `process.cwd()`: `cwd(): str`, the absolute path every relative path in a `std::fs` or `std::build` call resolves against. Deliberately NOT included: a project-root discovery helper (walk up to `vilan.toml`) — a real, separate decision this binding records and does not preempt. Docs `std/process.md`; the demand case is kolt (`proposals/projects/kolt.local` tracker item 018).

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::fs::read_dir_all` — the recursive directory scan.** `read_dir` is deliberately flat (the F13 v1 boundary, recorded in its own comment), so walking a tree meant `read_dir` + hand-joined paths + `stat` per entry to find the subdirectories + recursing — N+1 async round trips of manual bookkeeping, and exactly the dance the first real server wrote by hand to build its static asset table at boot. `read_dir_all(path)` is one call riding the host `readdir`'s `{ recursive: true }` option: every entry under `path`, files and subdirectories alike, as paths RELATIVE to `path` (joined with the host's own separator), in no promised order — `read_dir`'s honesty about ordering, recursively, and the same throwing posture on a missing or unreadable directory. Still no dirent-kind distinction: a caller that needs file vs. directory calls `stat`. The option object is host-side glue (`__fs_read_dir_all`, the `__fs_stat` seam) because the extern binding forms cannot spell it; the host's `readdir` must know the option (node ≥ 18.17 — the pin proves it against the real host). Docs `std/process.md`; `read_dir` stays flat, its comment now pointing here. Demand case: kolt (`proposals/projects/kolt.local` tracker item 019); F13's v1 fence, fullstack-dx.md §9.3.

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::style::declare` — the CSS escape hatch apps were hand-rolling becomes a first-class spelling.** The steer behind it: *"I am reimplementing the css emission logic to support the selectors I needed. Ideally this capability would be a part of the core (generically)."* Reading the app showed the demand is narrower and cleaner than "selectors" — it does not want a combinator, it wants a **set of declarations under a selector it chose**, minting no class and producing no `Style`, and std had no spelling for that at all. The whole hand-rolled hatch was one `emit("css", …)` plus a `.replace(";\n--", ";--")`, and that string surgery existed only because the asset channel is line-granular: it appends a LINE, deduplicates by line and sorts by line, so ten declarations written as a readable multi-line string had to be flattened before they could be emitted. `declare(selector, body)` builds the one line itself and the surgery has nothing left to do. `declarations()` opens a chain the way `style()` does — `.raw(property, value)` for any declaration (a custom property, a reset's `box-sizing`, a data URI whose `;` deliberately rides), and `.color`/`.length` for the typed values, which carry a token's own `:root` line onto the sheet exactly as a `Style` property does, so a ramp or spacing token spent in a block is never a dangling `var()`.

Ordering is settled by a cascade layer rather than a sort band: This is the first author-chosen text to enter the CSS channel, so its position had to be decided, and the sheet's existing band order is a consequence of the first byte (`*` < `.` < `:` < `@`) — which an author's own selector would land in arbitrarily. Every block therefore emits inside one layer, `@layer vilan`, and that is the whole rule: unlayered styles beat layered ones whatever their specificity, so **a `Style` always wins against a declaration block** — a block cannot reach in and out-specify a view's own rules however specific the selector it names, and where its line lands in the sort decides nothing. That is a stated, checkable invariant rather than a byte-order convention, it needs no change to the assembly path (a `@layer` line carries no `min-width`, so B35's ascending media sort is untouched), and it is exactly what a preflight reset will want. The cost is stated too: a block cannot override an *unlayered* declaration either, std's own token lines included, so a theme declares its own custom properties and reads them back with `Color::var` — which is the recipe the apps already use. **Validation is const-time and names its fix**, following the module's existing `check_*` precedent: a selector may not be blank, carry a newline (it would not indent the rule, it would split it into two independently sorted lines), carry a brace (`declare` writes those), or be an at-rule — `declare` puts declarations under a selector, and a group at-rule holds rules, not declarations; a block must declare something; and a property may not carry the `:` or `;` it owns.

It mints no class, joins no `Style`, alters no slot key and rehashes nothing**, which is what keeps this beside the atomic system rather than inside it — and the proof is in bytes: the corpus stylesheet gained exactly three lines and changed none, its `.mjs` twin is byte-identical, and every class name in the tree is where it was. One boundary is pinned in the opposite direction: `vilan fmt` never reorders a `declarations()` chain, because its links are cascade text joined in authoring order where a `style()` chain's links each own a slot. Docs `std/style.md`. (kolt.local 032)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`std::path` exists — `join`, `basename`, `dirname`, `extname`, `stem`, `normalize`, `resolve`, `relative`, `strip_prefix` — so `std::fs`'s own advice to "join with `path` yourself" stops naming a module that was never there.** That doc comment has been in `read_dir` since the filesystem surface grew, and it was the tree's own admission of the gap: there was no path type, no path module, and no way to address a directory entry except by hand-concatenating an interpolated string. Everything downstream did it that way — std's build layer, `std::document`'s file-name arithmetic, and, in the dogfood app, both a `scan_dir` join and a set of static-asset URL keys derived by cutting substrings.

Three forks were open when the item was filed and each is settled with its argument, in the module's own header where the next reader will find it. The first is the shape: free functions over `str`, not a `Path` type. `proposal/filesystem.md` §9 states the seam this lands against — `std::fs` "produces and consumes plain `str` paths and takes no position on a `Path` type" — and every one of its roughly thirty signatures takes `path: str`, so a type today would have to convert at all of them or change all of them, and changing them collides with that paper's unruled handle tier. The two functions supposed to earn the type back, `normalize` and `relative`, turned out to be a dozen lines each as free functions, so it does not earn it; a later `Path` can wrap a `str` and delegate to exactly these, and until then there is one representation of a path in the language rather than two. The second fork is separators, and it is a correctness call rather than a Unix preference: `/` is the separator on every platform, because a `join` that emitted `\` on Windows would make every path a program derives — a cache key, an asset URL, a golden — differ by host, which is the exact class `windows-support.md` §1 spent its length removing from the compiler, and Win32 accepts `/` in ordinary filesystem APIs anyway. What is not modelled is said rather than half-supported: drive-absolute and UNC paths are not roots, `\` is an ordinary character (it is a legal filename byte on Linux), and `fs::read_dir_all`'s entries carry the HOST separator, so on Windows they are not in this module's shape — a note that is now in `read_dir_all`'s own comment. Nothing folds case anywhere, which is the one thing this tooling could actually have done to undermine `windows-support.md` §5's case-exact module resolution, and it is pinned rather than merely intended. The third fork is URL paths, and they are out: they share the `/`-separated core and nothing else, since `extname("/app.js?v=2")` is `.js?v=2` under a file rule and `.js` under a URL rule, `%2F` is not a separator, and a URL's root is an origin. The seam is that a URL's own grammar comes off first and what is left is an ordinary path — which is what `std::document`'s `file_name_of` now is, two lines over `path::basename` instead of a hand-indexed `split("/")`.

The answers were differentialled against node's `path.posix` over a 34-case table before any of them was pinned, and they agree case for case with two deliberate divergences. `normalize` drops a trailing separator where node keeps it, because the whole point of a canonical form is that two spellings of one path compare equal and node's `"a/b"` and `"a/b/"` do not — which is how a cache or an asset map ends up with two entries for one file. And `dirname("a//b")` is `"a"` where node answers `"a/"`, a parent path carrying a dangling separator that whoever receives it has to normalize away. Everything else matches, including the corner the item's brief singled out: a dotfile has no extension, so `extname(".gitignore")` is `""` and not `".gitignore"` — the leading dot marks the file hidden, it does not name a type, and this is the single most common bug in hand-rolled path code. `extname` carries its leading dot so that `stem(p) + extname(p) == basename(p)` holds for every `p`, degenerate spellings included.

Two properties fall out of the module being plain vilan with no host call in it, and both are load-bearing rather than incidental. It is colorless: it sits in std's base `root` layer rather than `src/process`, so it serves a browser build as well as a server one, which matters because a browser router and an SSR render manipulate the same `/`-separated strings and a `@process`-colored path module would have put that shared half out of reach of half the program. And it is const-evaluable, so `const path::join("dist", "../dist/app.js")` folds to the literal `"dist/app.js"` in the emitted JavaScript — the property a build-time consumer needs, and one a `node:path` binding could never have had. Both are pinned, and both pins were shown to fail when the property is removed (the module moved into the process layer; a host call planted in `normalize`).

`join` and `resolve` are deliberately not the same verb. An absolute second argument does not reset `join` — `join("/a", "/b")` is `/a/b` — because `join` is textual, and the caller asking "…unless the second one is already absolute" is asking about references and wants `resolve`, where `resolve("/a", "/b")` is `/b`. `resolve` takes its base explicitly and has no working-directory default: node's falls back to `process.cwd()`, and taking a host fact would have colored the whole module for one function's convenience. Beside them, `path::starts_with` answers the question `str::starts_with` gets wrong for paths — `"/a/bc".starts_with("/a/b")` is `true` about the text and false about the filesystem — and `path::strip_prefix` is the cut that goes with that test, which is the other half of the Order 12 question: `str::strip_prefix` answers `Some("c")` for that same pair, so the `str` verbs do not suffice and the path sibling ships. It is a different question from `relative`, which always has an answer for two paths under one root and will climb with `..` to reach it, where `strip_prefix` is "is this mine, and if so what do I call it" and says `None` when it is not; `relative` itself returns `Option<str>` too, `None` in the two cases with no lexical answer at all — the two sides disagreeing about being absolute, or a `from` that still begins with `..` after the common prefix comes off. There is no path `strip_suffix`, because the only trailing affix a path commonly carries is a file extension and removing that is `stem`.

Pinned as eighteen cases rather than eighteen examples, each one an edge that bites: trailing separators in all four verbs that see them, `.` and `..` including a climb above an absolute root (dropped — the root's parent is the root) and a relative climb (kept — it names something), the empty sides of `join`, the dotfile rule and the dot-in-a-directory rule for `extname`, the `stem`/`extname` identity, the component-vs-text contrast for `starts_with` spelled out in one program beside the `str` answer, `relative`'s inversion of `resolve` and both of its `None` cases, and case-exactness. Every one was proven non-vacuous by planting the bug it removes and watching that pin go red — twenty-one plants in all, twenty of which reddened the pin they targeted. The twenty-first is worth recording because it is the one that did not: planting a `print` inside `normalize` does NOT cost the module its const-eligibility, so the const pin stayed green and a real host call (`random::range_i32`) had to be planted instead, which fails it with "const evaluation failed: `__random_int` is not available at expansion time". What did NOT move: `std/src/build.vl` still hand-concatenates `i"{dist}/{name}"`, which is the migration this module was written for and belongs to whoever lands the bundled-resource work in that file. Docs `std/paths.md`. (kolt.local 017)

---

<!-- family: feature -->
**`raw` takes a typed value now, so a theme token no longer has to be spent as bare text — which was dropping the `:root` line that defines it.** `Style::raw` and `Declarations::raw` were `(property: str, value: str)`, and every value the styling surface actually models is not a `str`. A `Length` or a `Color` is a pair: the text that goes in the declaration (`var(--space-4)`) and the `:root` line that declares the custom property that text refers to (`:root{--space-4:1rem}`). The typed methods have always carried both — `with_length`, `with_color`, `Declarations::color`, `Declarations::length` each put the second line on the sheet beside the first. `raw` had nowhere to put it, so the only way to get `space(4)` into a property the typed surface does not cover was to reach for its `.css` field, which hands over the text and leaves the declaration behind. The emitted stylesheet then referenced a custom property nothing in the build defined: `padding:var(--space-4)` with no `--space-4` anywhere, a dangling `var()` that resolves to the property's initial value and silently renders wrong. Reproduced before it was fixed, on the shipped surface, in one line: `style().raw("padding", space(4).css)` emitted the rule and no token line at all.

Both methods are now generic over a `CssValue` trait implemented by `str`, `Length` and `Color` — the `Slot`/`AttrValue` shape the element syntax introduced, applied on the style side. `str` is a complete CSS value written verbatim and declares nothing, which is every call site that exists today, unchanged and inferred. `Length` and `Color` carry their token's `:root` line onto the sheet exactly as the typed property methods do, so `.raw("padding", space(4))` and `.raw("outline-color", Color::blue(300))` are writable in a chain and are never a dangling `var()`. `with_length`, `with_color`, `Declarations::color` and `Declarations::length` are now literally `raw` at those value types rather than four near-copies of its body, and the pin that they mint byte-identical rules is what holds that true. The wrong-type diagnostic is the trait one: `'i32' does not implement trait 'CssValue', required by a generic bound of this call`, with a secondary span at the bound. Reaching for `.css` still hands over text and still declares nothing — that is what asking for the text alone means, and it is pinned as the contrast rather than papered over — but it is now a choice instead of the only spelling, and both book pages say to pass the value.

The trait describes a value rather than emitting for it: `css_text` and `css_root` are accessors, and the surface that writes the sheet does the writing. That split was forced, and the forcing is worth recording. `emit` is the compile-time channel, and the const-only capability check that fences it walks CALL EDGES — which it cannot follow through a bounded generic's trait dispatch. An `emit` inside the `Length` impl would therefore have escaped the check entirely: probed on the repo compiler, a generic function calling a trait method that emits compiles clean outside `const` and reaches the emitted JS as a live `__emit_asset` call with no runtime binding, a `ReferenceError` where the concrete spelling of the same call is a clean compile-time diagnostic. That gap predates this work and is pinned `#[ignore]`d with the reason. The same reasoning shaped `Declarations`, which took the `Gradient` route already used in this file: the chain accumulates the `:root` lines it owes and `declare` puts them on the sheet with the block, so a declaration chain stays ordinary runtime code and only `declare` is compile-time-only. An `emit` inside `Declarations::raw` would have made every declaration chain, `str` ones included, const-only. A chain that is never declared now emits nothing at all, tokens included, which is the honest reading of a block that was built and dropped.

Fifteen pins across both surfaces, each proven non-vacuous by planting the defect it guards: the token line carried and dropped on each of `Style` and `Declarations`; the empty-root guard, which without it writes a blank line into the sheet; the `str` instantiation left byte-identical; `raw` and `with_length` minting one rule; conditions and family last-wins surviving the widening; the two trait-bound diagnostics; the declaration checks reading the resolved text rather than the un-widened argument; and the chain's runtime-buildability, which the rejected inline-`emit` shape takes red. The corpus goldens and the emitted stylesheet are byte-identical — the widening's cost on the emitted JS was re-measured against the repo compiler before committing, per the paper's requirement, and is symbol renaming only. Docs `guide/styling.md` and `std/style.md`. (kolt.local 016, `proposal/css-block.md` §6, slice S1)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The parser refuses a too-deeply-nested file now instead of dying on it, and the stack margins that were standing in for that bound have come down with it.** Every recursive family in the *analyzer* was bounded in v0.36.0 and v0.37.0 (B138, B139), and each time the 256 MiB compiler-thread spawns and the wasm build's 64 MiB `-zstack-size` kept their values, because measuring the whole pipeline rather than the analyzer alone kept naming the same remaining consumer: the **parser**, which recurses once per level of source nesting with the largest frame in the pipeline and had no limit at all. It also runs first, so it reached the stack cliff before any analyzer bound could refuse — which is why the existing 5000-level pin had to use a method chain, a shape that is deep to the walk and flat to the parser. Nest the source syntactically instead and the file died with a stack overflow and no diagnostic whatsoever, the one outcome the depth work exists to prevent.

The bound does not live where it looked like it should. `parse_atom` is where the depth instrument was first hung and where the bracketed forms — `(…)`, `[…]`, a call's arguments, an index — all re-enter, so a limit there would have looked complete; it would have covered four doors and missed the rest. Within expressions the block-bearing forms (`{ … }`, `if`, `for`, `match`, a closure body) reach a nested expression without ever touching an atom, and so do the unary prefixes and the `const` prefix, which recurse into themselves. Past expressions there turned out to be five more recursive grammars, each a closed cycle that reaches no expression rule at all and so could not have been seen from anywhere in the expression grammar: types (`& & & … i64`, `[[…; 1]; 1]`, `L<L<…>>`), binders and patterns, items (nested `fun`, `mod`, `impl`, `trait`, and chained `export`), `import`/`use` paths, and nested elements. These were not theoretical. `fun a() { fun a() { … } }` at 5000 levels overflowed a 64 MiB worker outright, and the other four parsed 5000 levels clean with no refusal at all. So the guard sits on a shared funnel that all six grammars call through, and it counts **one** budget of 500 rather than six of them: what a stack margin needs bounded is the parser's total recursion depth, and six per-family counters of 500 would have admitted 3000 nested frames between them, which is no bound at all for the purpose.

Two details are load-bearing and were each arrived at the hard way. The refusal is held **off** the error list until the parse ends, because the backtracking combinator truncates that list whenever a branch declines — and refusing hands the enclosing rule a hole where it wanted an operand, so that rule's `)` goes missing, it declines, and the refusal would have been rolled back with it, leaving a file that was refused for depth reporting only a puzzled "expected `)`". How deep the input went is a fact about the input, not a claim by whichever branch happened to be exploring when it got there. And past the bound each grammar hands back a stand-in rather than declining, because declining sends 500 open frames back through their remaining alternatives: measured, refusing that way took nested `fun` at 505 levels from 9 ms to not finishing in 25 seconds. The two funnels whose callers loop on success consume a token as they refuse, which is what keeps those loops advancing instead of spinning. The refusal fires once per parse, not once per level — a 5000-level file gets one sentence, not 4500.

The number is 500, the same as the analyzer's walk, and it is now backed by a sweep rather than a guess: across all 211 compilable corpus entries the parser peaks at 23 levels with a median of 14, so the bound is 21.7× the deepest real file. What it buys is a finite worst case where there was none — measured through the CLI on the worst plant, a bounded parse peaks at depth 501 and 35.2 MiB unoptimized, about 10 MiB optimized — and, because the parser will not descend past the limit, it cannot *build* a tree deeper than it either, so every later walk over the AST is bounded by construction rather than needing a bound of its own. On that basis the **compiler-thread spawns drop from 256 MiB to 128 MiB** (the CLI's and the language server's), roughly 3.6× the measured worst case with room left for a macro-world compile, which nests a whole pipeline inside the running analysis. The **wasm build's stack drops from 64 MiB to 16 MiB**, and that one was measured under the profile that actually ships rather than extrapolated from native frame sizes: the `wasm-release` artifact (`opt-level = "z"` plus fat LTO) was built at a range of stack sizes and run under node against the same pathological plants, where 8 MiB compiles every one of them and 4 MiB traps on the two worst. That is 48 MiB of linear memory the playground page no longer reserves before it can do anything.

Pinned per door: twenty-one plants, one for each way source can nest a level deeper, each confirmed to overflow or hang before the bound existed and each asserting the same three claims — a tree still comes out, exactly one refusal, and it steers toward the flattening that fits the construct. The formatter's own parse mode is pinned separately, since it is a second entry into the same parser and a bound wired into one entry point would have left `vilan fmt` overflowing on a file `vilan build` refuses cleanly. (backlog B142)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The release path stops trusting mutable tags, and the installer stops installing what it cannot verify.** Two independent holes in the supply chain, both found by the first security audit run, both closed the same way — by removing a place where the toolchain trusted something it had not checked. Every third-party action in `ci.yml` and `release.yml` rode a mutable tag: `actions/checkout@v4`, `dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable`, `taiki-e/install-action@v2`, the artifact pair, `azure/login@v2`, `actions/create-github-app-token@v2`. A git tag is a pointer its owner can move, so each of those was a standing grant of arbitrary code execution to whoever could move it — and in the release workflow that code runs holding the release token, next to the npm trusted-publishing exchange and the Azure OIDC login. All 32 `uses:` lines are now pinned to full commit SHAs with the tag in a trailing comment (`uses: actions/checkout@11d5960a… # v4.4.0`), which is the form that stays readable while being immutable. Every SHA was resolved from the real upstream and cross-checked two ways, against `git ls-remote` with tags peeled and against the API with annotated tags dereferenced; none was taken from memory. `dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable` deserves its own note, because pinning it looks like it should freeze the Rust version and does not: `stable` is a branch, and its `action.yml` defaults the `toolchain` input to the literal string `stable`, which is a rustup channel name resolved at run time — so the pin freezes the action's code and rustup still installs whatever stable is that morning. That was verified by reading the branch's `action.yml`, not assumed.

A pin with nothing tending it is only a frozen dependency, and a frozen action stops receiving its own security fixes, so `.github/dependabot.yml` lands with the pins as one measure rather than two — Dependabot reads the tag comment beside each SHA and rewrites both when it opens a weekly bump PR, grouped into a single reviewable diff. Separately, `scripts/install.sh` failed open: when neither `sha256sum` nor `shasum` was on `PATH` it printed a warning, skipped verification entirely, and unpacked the download anyway, exiting 0 with the full success banner. That put the weakest verification on precisely the machine least able to notice, and an installer that only sometimes verifies makes a promise it does not keep. It now refuses, in its own voice on stderr with a non-zero exit, naming the fix — every supported platform can satisfy it, since `sha256sum` ships with GNU coreutils and `shasum` with macOS and with perl — and "skipping" is no longer an outcome it offers on either stream. Note the limit of what this buys, recorded rather than glossed: `sha256sums.txt` is produced and consumed from the same release page, so it authenticates the transfer and not the pipeline that produced it. Signing and provenance are a separate, larger piece of work and stay open. The same sweep is owed to the two sibling repositories and goes with it: the proposals repo's `hygiene.yml` declared no `permissions:` block and so inherited the default token grant for a job that only reads, and the website's `deploy.yml` plus `fetch-wasm.sh` piped the playground's WebAssembly compiler straight from `curl` into `tar` with no checksum at all — bytes that then execute in every visitor's browser. Both now carry the same discipline. (backlog L14, L15's S half, N18, K19)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Inferring a return type through a long call chain is bounded now, and no longer quadratic.** When a function declares no return type, working out what it returns means working out what the function it calls returns, and so on down the chain — one level of recursion per call link, carrying a large frame (~12.8 KiB per link unoptimized, ~2.2 KiB optimized). Nothing bounded that: the guard in place caught *cycles*, not depth, so a long enough chain of unannotated returns could exhaust the stack. Two things change. The chain is now **bounded at 500 links**, 25x deeper than any real code reaches, and a program past it gets one clean diagnostic — "declare a return type on one of them to end the chain", which is the actual fix, since a declared return is read off the signature and ends the recursion — instead of an overflow. And the answer for each function is now **reused** rather than re-derived: an exact answer was already being recorded, but only a read-only coercion path ever read it, so every link in a chain re-walked every link beneath it. A 500-function chain went from 390,984 inference entries to 14,730, and the cost is now a straight line in the chain's length rather than a curve. In the common case where a function is defined after the ones it calls, the recursion now flattens away entirely: the same 500-function chain went from 502 levels deep and 6.26 MiB of stack to 6 levels and 0.07 MiB. **The stack margins still do not shrink, and now say exactly why.** The 256 MiB compiler-thread spawns and the wasm build's 64 MiB `-zstack-size` were waiting on this bound; with it landed, every recursive family in the *analyzer* is bounded (~3.4 MiB together at release frame sizes). But measuring the whole pipeline rather than the analyzer alone — `VILAN_DEPTH_STATS` now covers the parser too, and anchors before the parse instead of after it — turned up a deeper consumer: the **parser** recurses once per level of source nesting at ~20.3 KiB per level optimized, four times the bounded expression walk's cost, with no depth limit at all, and it runs first, so it reaches the cliff before any analyzer bound can refuse. That is what the margins are now standing in for, and each site says so. The depth line also reports a per-family call count, which is what made the quadratic behaviour visible. (backlog B139)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Deeply nested expressions no longer take exponential time to parse.** `(1 + (1 + (1 + …)))` at 22 levels of nesting took 35 seconds to compile, 60 levels never finished, and nested array literals behaved the same way — a size a generated file or a macro expansion reaches without trying. The cost was in the PARSER, and it doubled with every level: an assignment (`place = value`) is tried before ordinary expressions, and the only way to know whether one is present was to parse the whole left-hand side speculatively and throw it away when no `=` turned up — after which the ordinary-expression path parsed the identical text again. Every bracket level therefore paid for its own contents twice, which is 2^n. The parser now works out up front, in one pass over the tokens, which positions could still be followed by an assignment operator — a place is a balanced run of tokens, so its operator always sits at the bracket depth the place began at — and skips the speculative parse where none can be. 20 levels went from 9.0s to 0.24ms; 320 levels now compile in a quarter of a second, and the cost is a straight line in the nesting depth. Nothing about what parses, or what any diagnostic says, changes. The measurement that found it is worth recording, because it was mis-filed as a type-inference bug: the analyzer's own phase clocks stayed flat near 200ms while the whole analysis took 8.4 seconds, and the recursion depth never moved — all of it was spent before analysis began. A pin holds nested expressions to a constant number of parses per level, counting parser work rather than wall-clock so that a regression to merely quadratic is caught too. (backlog B140)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Organize Imports models usage as what the file actually resolved, so it stops removing imports the build needs and stops keeping ones it doesn't.** Both reported failures were the same defect seen from opposite sides: "used" meant *syntactically mentioned somewhere in this file*, which is neither necessary nor sufficient.

Not sufficient, so it **over-pruned**: `import pkg::helper;` where the file's only debt to it is an `impl` method it calls — `n.doubled()`, with the module's name never written — looked entirely unused and was removed, and the file stopped building. That is now answered from the analyzer's own provenance: a whole-module import is kept when this file resolved *anything declared in the file that import reaches into*, methods included. The question is asked of the reference index, so a method call, a constructor, an enum variant and a type annotation are all one query rather than four hand-written scans.

Not necessary, so it **under-pruned** — and the real culprit is sharper than reported. `Ok` and `Err` on their own were pruned correctly all along; the leaf that never pruned was `self`. An import path's segments resolve to the very definitions its leaves bind, so in `import std::result::Result::{ self, Ok, Err };` the `Result` written in the path counted as a use of `Result`, and the `self` leaf could therefore never be removed no matter what the file did — an import that justifies its own existence. References written by the file's **import list** are now excluded from the accounting, so an import must be earned by the code.

The pin the item asked for is in place: a build-preservation corpus that starts green, requires organize to actually change the text (a case organize no-ops on would pass vacuously and prove nothing), and requires the organized text to analyze green again — over a module import reached only through an impl method, an unused module import beside a used one, a brace set with one live and one dead leaf, an unused variant import beside a used one, and a shuffled run where everything is used. Over-pruning surfaces there as a diagnostic and under-pruning as an unchanged text, so one pin holds both directions. (kolt.local tracker item 004)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Rename is a thin layer over the reference index, and refuses with a reason rather than emitting a partial edit.** The two reported symptoms turned out to be **two distinct bugs sharing one root class**, and their different wording was the clue. Renaming a struct field *failed outright* — "Rename failed to apply edits" is the client's message, not the server's, and it meant the request had succeeded and returned an inapplicable `WorkspaceEdit`: a cursor on a field's declaration resolved to the enclosing *struct*, and a struct's constructor sites were emitted twice over, once from the type-reference table and once from the initializer table, so the client rejected the whole overlapping edit. Renaming a static method was merely *"problematic"* — that edit set was perfectly applicable and simply wrong, because a `Type::method()` call's recorded span is the whole path and the declaration it paired with was the whole `fun … { … }`, so accepting the rename overwrote the entire function declaration with the new name. One class — occurrences reconstructed span-by-span per symbol kind — two failure modes, one inapplicable and one destructive.

Both are gone with the reference index behind them, and rename now adds only what rename needs beyond finding: that the new name is spellable, that every reference sits in a file the project may edit, and that nothing is known to be missing. It **refuses, with the reason, rather than emitting an incomplete set** — a half-applied rename does not fail loudly, it leaves a program that no longer builds and an editor that reported success. Renaming into a keyword or a non-identifier is refused (checked against the lexer's own keyword table, so it cannot drift from the language). Renaming a symbol declared in the standard library or a dependency is refused by name — reaching a `std` function through an import used to hand the client edits for files under `$VILAN_STD` without a word. A reference in `[derive(..)]`-generated code, which has no file on disk to edit, is refused rather than skipped, and so is any occurrence that cannot be resolved to a location. `textDocument/prepareRename` is now answered too, decided by exactly the checks the rename itself runs, so the editor can refuse before the user types a new name instead of after — and the two cannot disagree.

The per-symbol-kind matrix the item asked for is pinned: struct, field, instance method, static method, free function, enum, enum variant in expression and in pattern position, local, parameter — each asserting that rename rewrites *exactly* what find-references reports, that every edit replaces one identifier and never a declaration or a path, and that no span is ever emitted twice. (kolt.local tracker item 002)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**Find References answers every symbol kind, from one reference index.** It used to return nothing at all for an enum variant — clicked on its own declaration, or in a match pattern — and nothing but the declaration for a free function or a static method, while a cursor on a struct field's declaration silently answered with the *struct's* references and a cursor on a return-type annotation with the *enclosing function's*. Those look like six bugs and were one: the query guessed what the cursor named by asking which syntactic node contained it, and a node's span is the whole `fun … { … }` or the whole `struct { … }`, so a coarse declaration swallowed the precise probes underneath it. Having guessed, it then rebuilt each occurrence's span separately per symbol kind, out of whichever analyzer table sat nearest — which is how renaming a static method from its call site came to emit the entire function declaration as one span to overwrite, how `Enum::Variant` came to be rewritten whole rather than at its last segment, and how a struct's constructor sites came to be emitted twice, once from `type_references` and once from the initializer table.

Both halves are now one table. Each analysis builds a **reference index**: every identifier occurrence in the program, each a span covering exactly an identifier and the definition it names. The cursor is resolved by looking its offset up in that same table, so resolution and enumeration cannot disagree — a kind that can be found is necessarily one that can be enumerated, because both directions read one set of rows — and there is no coarse span left to shadow anything. Two invariants carry it and both are pinned: every row's text *is* its definition's name, and no two rows share a span. The first subsumes the whole whole-span class in a single assertion; the second is what makes an edit set applicable rather than overlapping. Where a `::` path carries no span for its member, the span is derived rather than searched — the parser ends a static-accessor node at the member's own end token, so the final `name.len()` bytes are the identifier exactly — and a span that cannot be *proven* to cover an identifier is dropped and counted, never emitted wrong, so an incomplete answer can be refused instead of quietly returned.

One gap in the analyzer had to close for this to be honest: the key `x` in `Point { x = 1 }` names the field, but it is not a field access, so unlike `p.x` it had nowhere to be recorded — the analyzer computed the span for a diagnostic and dropped it. Renaming a struct field therefore missed every construction site and produced a partial edit that broke the build. Those spans are now kept (`struct_initializer_field_spans`), and a field answers with its declaration, its accesses and its initializer keys alike. A per-symbol-kind matrix pins the lot — struct, field, instance method, static method, free function, enum, enum variant in both expression and pattern position, local, parameter — each from every end it can be clicked from, alongside a consistency pin that edits, queries, edits again and queries, so an answer tracks the buffer both ways.

One limit is now recorded rather than merely suffered, as an `#[ignore]`d pin: the query still answers over the open file's own program — its entry file plus everything it imports — so a symbol clicked in the file that *defines* it does not see the files that import that file. In a multi-file app that is most of the time, and it is the other half of what "intermittently returns nothing" described. Closing it means a definition key stable across programs and a union over the open set; the index makes that a lookup per program rather than a redesign. (kolt.local tracker item 003)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The HMR dev channel now requires a per-run token on every route.** Dev-only, and never a network surface: the channel binds `127.0.0.1` alone, and its artifact routes always refused anything but a bare `<name>.<ext>`. What was open was the *browser*. While `run --watch` ran, any other page the developer happened to visit could reach the fixed default port `35917` and read `GET /bundle/<leg>.js` (the compiled app), hold `GET /events` open and watch every compile failure arrive as it was typed — the overlay payload carries `file:line:col`, the diagnostic text, and requirement-trace hops — and fire `POST /refresh` for a reload flood. `run --watch` now mints 128 bits per process and substitutes them into the browser shim exactly as it already substitutes the port; `/events`, `/bundle/`, `/asset/` and `/refresh` all refuse a missing or wrong token with a bare `403` that says nothing about which it was. The legitimate page has the token because the bundle carrying it came from the page's own origin; a hostile page cannot read that bundle and so can neither fetch nor forge. `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` deliberately stays — the app's origin is the user's own server and genuinely unknowable, and an origin allowlist would not have closed the refresh flood anyway, since a simple cross-origin POST needs no CORS permission. `std::watch::force_refresh()` is the one legitimate non-browser caller and learns the token from `VILAN_HMR_TOKEN`, inherited at spawn beside `VILAN_HMR_PORT`; its behavior is unchanged. Nothing to configure. One visible consequence: a tab left open from a previous watch session carries that session's token, so after restarting `run --watch` you reload it once. Docs `guide/dev-loop.md`. (backlog E93, N16 audit run 1)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan fmt` puts a `style()` chain's links in a canonical order — Tailwind's, with your own methods held in place.** A long builder chain read differently in every file, and the order carries no meaning of its own: a chain merges last-wins per property slot, so links on independent slots may be written in any order at all. `vilan fmt` now settles it — Tailwind CSS's category sequence (layout, flexbox/grid, spacing, sizing, typography, backgrounds, borders, effects, filters, tables, transitions/animation, transforms, interactivity, svg, accessibility), with every condition combinator after every property method in the axis order the selector already nests them (`md`, `dark`, `attribute`, pseudo-class). This is the only place `fmt` reorders code rather than re-laying it out, so it is correct by construction rather than by table lookup. A method the formatter does not know — one of your own `impl Style` extensions, or an escape hatch whose slot is an argument (`raw`, `with_length`, `with_color`, `with_border`) — is a **barrier**: links sort only within the runs between barriers, nothing crosses one, and a chain of nothing but your own methods is left exactly as written. Methods whose slots are entangled share a **family** and keep their written order, because `padding` then `padding_x` means something the reverse does not (`proposal/ui-styling.md` §0bis) — `padding` over its edges, `size` over `width`/`height`, `border` over `border_color`. Only independent slots ever cross, and the proof is the corpus itself: every tracked style source builds to a **byte-identical** stylesheet before and after the reorder, with the same slots resolving to the same declarations. The order table is derived from `std/src/style.vl` and gated against it in four directions, so a new style method is a red test rather than a link that quietly stops sorting. Docs `appendix/cli.md`. (kolt.local 006)

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**CI runs on a read-only token, and every Windows FFI `unsafe` site carries its `SAFETY:` label.** Two tidies from the first recurring codebase audit (N16, security dimension). `ci.yml` never writes anything, but declared no `permissions:` and so inherited whatever the repository default grants — it now claims `contents: read` explicitly, the narrowing `release.yml` has always done per job. And the seven sound-but-unlabeled `unsafe` sites in `vilan-cli`'s Windows console/job-object FFI (`paint.rs`, `job.rs`) now state their safety arguments in the workspace's `SAFETY:` convention, which every other `unsafe` site already follows — the audit verified each argument against the code; no behavior changes.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The grammar word lists are generated, not copied.** `grammar_sync` graduates from gating the static grammars' word lists to generating them: the keyword, primitive-type, attribute-marker, numeric-suffix and operator fragments of the TextMate grammar and the book's highlight.js theme are now emitted from the compiler's exported tables (`KEYWORDS`, the newly exported `TWO_CHARACTER_OPERATORS`, `SCALAR_PRIMITIVE_NAMES`, `NUMERIC_SUFFIXES`, `KNOWN_ATTRIBUTE_MARKERS`) and byte-held in place on every suite run — a table changed without regenerating (`VILAN_REGENERATE_GRAMMARS=1 cargo test -p vilan-cli --test grammar_sync generated`) is a red suite, not a reader's eye. The structural rules stay hand-written, and the same seam is where the tree-sitter grammar (backlog E62) will hang when its lane opens — born gated.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The analyzer's recursion depth is measured now, and its deepest path is bounded.** The stack margins around the compiler were sized by incident, not measurement — the v0.36.0 gate SIGABRT'd when a modest server program's analysis outgrew a CI worker's ~2 MiB stack. `VILAN_DEPTH_STATS` answers the question that incident left open, the `VILAN_PHASE_TIMING` way: set it and every top-level analysis prints one stderr line with, per recursive family (`infer`, `type-walk`, `expr-walk`, `pattern`), the peak recursion depth and the stack consumed at that peak. What it measured: every realistic fixture — both walkthrough entries, the std twin-parity and release-emission corpora, the incident's own server program — peaks at 20 levels, and the deepest path by bytes is the phase-1 expression walk at ~36 KiB per nesting level unoptimized (~4.5 KiB optimized), one frame per level of syntactic nesting, unguarded. That walk is now depth-bounded at 500 levels — 25× the deepest realistic fixture — and an expression nesting past it gets one clean diagnostic steering toward `let`-flattening instead of a stack overflow: a 5000-deep method chain that aborted a 64 MiB worker (SIGABRT) now refuses in under a second. The 256 MiB worker spawns and the wasm build's 64 MiB `-zstack-size` keep their values but lose their folklore status — each site now carries the measured rationale, and what keeps them generous is named precisely: cross-function return-inference chains (~12.5 KiB per call link unoptimized) are not yet bounded.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The walkthrough example spells its sentinel `-1`.** The four rpc bodies in its store (and the guide fence that mirrors one of them) answered an unauthenticated call with `0 - 1`, though prefix `-` has sat in the grammar's operator table all along — and a teaching surface teaches: the first downstream refactor written against the example carried the long spelling verbatim into its own service. Swept to `-1`; the guide and the example stay mirrored, and the fence gate and example builds hold.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`std::crypto`'s promoted primitives are documented.** `hmac_sha512` and `pbkdf2_sha512` shipped in std, but the misc page's crypto section still listed only `random_bytes`/`random_uuid`/`equals_constant_time` and sent readers off to "bind the host's sync primitives as externs … candidates for std promotion" — teaching a workaround for a need std now covers. The section lists both promoted functions and keeps the extern lesson for what it is still for: std's surface is async because WebCrypto is, so a path that must stay sync (the walkthrough's rpc dispatch hashes passwords inside a sync method) still binds Node's `pbkdf2Sync`, exactly as the walkthrough example does. Same sweep, same class: `content_type_of`'s doc comment no longer claims `std::fs` cannot read bytes — stale since `read_bytes` shipped.

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`serve_build` says which artifact it will not serve.** The fence stands as ruled (`fullstack-dx.md` §5.10): `serve_build` serves a build, not a directory, and an artifact whose extension the content-type table does not name is not served rather than guessed at. But the drop was silent — `load_build`'s unknown-extension arm was `None => {}`, so such an artifact simply 404'd through to the app's fallback for the life of the process with nothing anywhere saying why. Boot now prints a warning naming the leg, the file, and the consequence: `` warning: the `client` build names dist/logo.png, whose extension `serve_build` has no content type for — the artifact is not served ``. Fence-neutral by construction: no mime types were added and what is served is unchanged — the table's own policy question (completeness vs. extensibility) stays open, and this warning does not preempt it. kolt (`proposals/projects/kolt.local` tracker item 022, ask (b)).

<!-- family: tooling -->
**An operator at an all-native binding no longer ICEs when the impl's body calls the trait method explicitly.** A conditional impl whose body spells the comparison `.eq()` instead of `==` — `impl Pair<type T: PartialEq> with PartialEq { fun eq(self, b: Pair<T>): bool { self.a.eq(b.a) && self.b.eq(b.b) } }` — died in an internal error at `Pair<i32> == Pair<i32>`: "a call resolved to `PartialEq`'s requirement `eq`, which has no body … please report this program" (B127's family, the OPERATOR half). The operator lowering skipped monomorphization for all-native bindings on the assumption that the body uses only operators on `T`, which lower to native JS — an explicit `.eq()` instead fell through to the trait's bodyless requirement. `Pair<List<i32>> ==` and explicit `.eq()` calls always worked, and std's own impls dodge the shape by spelling their bodies with `==` (compare.vl's doc comment records it — now as history rather than a constraint). The analyzer now records the operand's type-arg bindings unconditionally — the same rule an explicit method call gets — and the transformer decides per site: a non-native binding specializes as before, and an all-native binding keeps the one shared generic emission UNLESS the body requires the substitution — a call that would otherwise land on a bodyless requirement, found transitively through the program's call graph (generic helpers, trait-default fallbacks, and lexical closures included), in which case the method monomorphizes. Every corpus golden is byte-identical: the shared emission remains the emitted shape for every operator-only body. Pinned red-first in all three shapes — direct, through a generic helper, and through a closure (B135, std-doc-smalls' report Q1).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**A `Workspace` staging a dependency edge named `std` or `pkg` is refused at the analyzer's own door.** The manifest layer has refused the names since v0.36.0 (L12), but a `Workspace` built programmatically — wasm, embedders, tests — could still stage one, and the shape had two answers for one name: the analyzer's `resolve_import_root` checks dependency edges first, so a dependency named `std` silently replaced the entire standard library, while the IDE's completion (and the macro world's scope resolution) answered the stdlib. `analyze` — the funnel every entry path passes through — now reports such an edge with the manifest's own reserved-name refusal and drops it rather than failing the program: `std::` and `pkg::` keep meaning the roots they always name, and the resolvers agree by construction because the shape no longer exists past the door. `macro_std` stays a legal edge name at this layer — the macro world itself stages the macro standard library as one; user manifests remain refused up front by L12 (E88, filed from L12's report Q3).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan build` warns when a superseded generation of an artifact survives beside the new one.** A project last built before v0.33.0's artifact rename (process legs `.js` → `.mjs`) rebuilds clean on the current toolchain — and the old `dist/server.js` keeps sitting beside the new `dist/server.mjs`, so a script, Dockerfile, or process manager still saying `node dist/server.js` keeps launching the superseded application silently, exactly the drift the gotchas page warns about with nothing in the toolchain to catch it. The build now checks, after writing each bundle, for a same-stem sibling under the other script classification's extension — within one build the `dist/<name>.*` namespace belongs to one leg, so a surviving `.js` beside a `.mjs` (or a stranded `.mjs` after a leg retargeted to the browser) can only be an earlier generation — and warns, naming both files and the fix. Warned, never swept: no record proves the build wrote the old file (pre-rename builds recorded nothing, which is also why a record-scoped sweep would ship blind to every dist/ already carrying the residue), and the output directory is not exclusively the build's, so user files are never touched. The lone-package form (`src/main.js` beside `src/main.mjs`) gets the same check at its beside-the-source write (E92; a whole-build emission record for the renamed-leg and dropped-leg shapes stays open in the tracker).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**The docs gate's heading-id twin can no longer drift from `std::markdown`'s rule.** The test-only reimplementation the keyword-hover deep links are checked against (`book_sync`'s `normalize_id`) lowercased ASCII-only and skipped the trim mdBook performs after dropping tags — both empirically wrong against mdBook v0.5.4 (`École Été` → `École-Été` where the renderer says `école-été`; an anchor-fronted heading kept the anchor's trailing space as a leading `-`), and both latent because no book heading exercises them today, which is exactly how a checking surface rots. The twin now trims after the tag drop and lowercases with the full Unicode fold, both shapes are pinned, and a differential gate holds the twin to the same `markdown_anchors.golden` (456 ids scraped from a real mdBook v0.5.4 build) that `std::markdown`'s `heading_id` already answers to — with both implementations reproducing one golden, neither can drift from the other over the book's ids without its own gate going red (B137, `proposal/markdown.md` §10.1).

---

<!-- family: tooling -->
**`vilan fmt` collapses a one-member brace import to its unbraced spelling.** `import a::{ b }` and `import a::b` spell the same import, and Format Document left the braced one standing — the shape hand-written code falls into whenever a set shrinks to one survivor. The unbraced form is now canonical, recursively (`a::{ b::{ c } }` reaches `a::b::c`) and for `use` too — the canonical form the tracker item asked to have defined. Two exceptions keep their braces: a lone `self` (`Option::{ self }` publishes `Option`; there is no unbraced spelling) and a set holding a comment (anchored to the split form). Organize Imports rides the same printer, so pruning a set down to one member renders the unbraced spelling as well, and the add-import quickfix never builds a one-member set — no editor path regenerates the braces. The shared sort key sees through the braces too (`unwrap_singleton_set`): the braced spelling ranks exactly where its collapsed form lands, so a reprint can never order a run against the safety net's canonicalization (the silent-bail interaction the corpus caught). The three corpus programs carrying the shape reflowed (`loops.vl`, `async-await.vl`, `async-promise-all.vl`); every emitted-JS golden is byte-identical — imports are spelling, not codegen. kolt (`proposals/projects/kolt.local` tracker item 005).
